Figure 2
:

Construction: Functional Adaptor Signatures

Our formal construction is as in
Figure

2
.
We defer the security theorem to
Section

5
.
We alluded to in ‘Our Technique 3’ on how to avoid NIZKs in interactive pre-signing by making non-blackbox use of the IPFE compiler. Having described the full details of FAS construction, we now elucidate on it in
Remarks

2.2
and
2.1
.

Remark 2.1

(Simulatability with leakage
f_{\bf y}({\bf t})
on
{\bf t}
)
.

Note that while proving zero-knowledge of FAS, the FAS simulator would still have to reveal
f_{\bf y}({\bf t})
.
This helps us avoid NIZKs in the interactive pre-signing as discussed before.
Also note that FAS simulator would use the IPFE simulator internally, and
f_{\bf y}({\bf t})
is a leakage on
the random coins
{\bf t}
of the IPFE (compiler’s) simulator.
Crucially, this does not break simulation security of IPFE.
This is because the IPFE compiler of
[
ALMT20
]
— and hence the IPFE simulator too – inherently leaks
f_{\bf y}({\bf t})
.
The latter is because
{{\sf IPFE}}
decryption implicitly takes
\widetilde{{\bf y}}
as input and hence, the decrypter needs to know
f_{\bf y}({\bf t})
to ensure decryption correctness.
Hence, from an honest seller’s perspective, the
\mathsf{FAS}
zero-knowledge simulation won’t be affected by giving away
f_{\bf y}({\bf t})
as part of
\pi_{\bf y}
.

Remark 2.2

(Purpose of
\pi_{\bf y}
)
.

Note that
\pi_{\bf y}
enables verifying that
{\sf aux}_{\bf y}
is functional public key corresponding to
\widetilde{{\bf y}}=({\bf y}^{T},\pi_{\bf y})^{T}
. Regarding
\widetilde{{\bf y}}
, the buyer knows
{\bf y}
but it does not know whether the last slot of
\widetilde{{\bf y}}
chosen by the seller is well-formed, i.e., is
\pi_{\bf y}=f_{\bf y}({\bf t})
?
In security against a malicious seller, we argue that we do not need to guarantee this.
In particular, it does not affect an honest buyer whether
\pi_{\bf y}=f_{\bf y}({\bf t})
holds or not. Suppose that the malicous seller chooses arbitrary value
{\sf val}
and sends
({\sf aux}_{\bf y},\pi_{\bf y})=(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y},{\sf val})
, where
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}={{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf PubKGen}({\sf mpk},\widetilde{{\bf y}})
and
\widetilde{{\bf y}}=({\bf y}^{T},{\sf val})^{T}
.
This would certainly ensure that
{\mathsf{AuxVerify}}
passes and the buyer continues the protocol with the seller. Crucially though, we argue that if the honest buyer does end up making the payment to the seller, then, the honest buyer indeed learns
f_{\bf y}({\bf x})
at the end of the protocol disregard of what
{\sf val}
was chosen by the malicious seller.
This is because
advertisement soundness
would guarantee the honest buyer that
{\sf ct}
encrypts a vector
\widetilde{{\bf x}}
of the form
\widetilde{{\bf x}}=({\bf x}^{T},0)^{T}
, where
(X,{\bf x})\in R
.
Further, witness extractability would guarantee that the honest buyer extracts a functional secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}
corresponding to
\widetilde{{\bf y}}=({\bf y}^{T},{\sf val})^{T}
. Thus, IPFE correctness would guarantee that the honest buyer recovers
f_{\widetilde{{\bf y}}}(\widetilde{{\bf x}})
which is same as
f_{\bf y}({\bf x})
since the last slot of
\widetilde{{\bf x}}
is zero.

2.4
Instantiations

In
Section

6
, we provide an instantiation from prime order groups for the function class of
inner products over integers with output values polynomially bounded by
B\ll p
.
For example, if each entry of the witness/function vector is bounded by
10^{3}
, and the vectors have
10^{6}
entries, then, the inner product value would be bounded by
10^{12}
, so we can set
B=10^{12}
. We explain below application scenarios where such restrictions are reasonable.
We also present an instantiation from lattices in
Section

7
that removes such restrictions.
Specifically, it works for the function class of inner products modulo prime integer
p
.
Further, it is post-quantum secure.

2.4.1
Prime-Order Groups based Instantiation.

To instantiate our FAS construction in
Figure

2
from prime order groups,
we instantiate the
\mathsf{AS}
as Schnorr adaptor signature scheme
[
EFH+21
]
w.r.t. the hard relation
R_{\mathsf{DL}}
, where
R_{\mathsf{DL}}
is the discrete-log relation in prime order
groups (see
Definition

3.3
).
We then set
R_{{\sf IPFE}}=R_{\mathsf{DL}}
and show that a varaiant of the selective, IND-secure IPFE scheme by Abdalla et al.
[
ABDP15
]
satisfies
R_{\mathsf{DL}}
-compliance.

Small plaintext space: reason and application scope.

This limitation is because the function output here is encoded in the exponent and functional extraction of FAS instantiation involves a discrete log computation step. For values in the exponent bounded by
B
, this incurs a running time of
O(\sqrt{B})
. This is efficient only if bound
B
is a polynomial.
We argue that this is sufficient for several realistic application scenarios. We benchmarked computation for datasets having a maximum of
10^{6}
entries, resulting in a 32 MB dataset. For example, the breast cancer dataset on Kaggle
[
kag
]
has
20000
entries, total size
50
KB, sum of entries
\approx 10^{6}
. Assuming each entry of a function is at most
10^{4}
, we get
B=10^{10}
. Thus, the benchmarks in
Table

2
for
(\ell=10^{4},B=10^{10})
fit closest for this dataset.
Other scenarios occur when the secret database is a company’s employee record with their age, years of service with the employer, retirement contribution, etc. Such values while sensitive, are typically bounded (e.g., by
B=10^{10}
) and the buyer may wish to learn statistical/aggregate information, like sum, weighted mean or average, etc. whose result is also in the bounded plaintext space. The buyer could be a research organization that is studying the workforce in companies or factories, or it could be a recruitment recommendation agency that tries to match clients with potential employers with appropriate aggregate requirements of the client.

2.4.2
Lattice based Instantiation.

We instantiate the
\mathsf{AS}
as the lattice-based adaptor signature scheme by Esgin et al.
[
EEE20a
]
w.r.t. the inhomogenous short integer solution relation

R_{{\sf ISIS}}
(see
Definition

3.18
).
Our instantiation is over the ring of integers
\mathcal{R}=\mathbb{Z}
. Consequently, its security follows from plain SIS and LWE assumptions.
Further, we show that a variant of the IND-secure IPFE scheme by Agrawal et al.
[
ALS16
, Section 4.2]
satisfies
R_{{\sf ISIS}}
-compliance.

7
7

7

Note that in lattice-based adaptor signature scheme by Esgin et al.
[
EEE20a
]
, the extracted witness does not satisfy
R_{{\sf ISIS}}
but it satisfies an extended relation
R^{\prime}_{{\sf ISIS}}
such that
R_{{\sf ISIS}}\subset R^{\prime}_{{\sf ISIS}}
. This is due to the rejection sampling step involved in these signatures. Using such signatures along with
{{\sf IPFE}}
thus becomes technically more challenging as the extracted witness acts as the
{{\sf IPFE}}
decryption key. So, we additionally require a decryption robustness property from
{{\sf IPFE}}
(
Definition

3.14
).

For technical reasons related to IPFE, the resulting FAS construction can only support linearly independent function request across all buyers (See
Remark

7.2
for details). This could be quite restrictive since the scheme cannot support same function requests from different buyers.
In
Section

7.4
,
we show how to modify the FAS construction to remove this restriction.

2.5
Towards Non-Interactive Pre-Signing

One of the main features of adaptor signatures that enable scalability is the non-interactive nature of the pre-signature generation algorithm. More specifically, the buyer in an adaptor signature execution can generate a pre-signature just from the seller’s advertisement and, more importantly, without further interaction with the seller. Since the exchange of pre-signatures is done off-chain and hence the latency doesn’t impact the blockchain eco-system, a non-interactive pre-signing phase is more preferable.

We relaxed pre-signing to be interactive to achieve strong witness privacy for FAS, namely, the malicious buyer learns no information about
{\bf x}
other than
f({\bf x})
.
This relaxation is not new to our work and was already introduced in
[
QPM+23
]
to achieve the advanced feature of
blindness
for adaptor signatures.
Despite numerous attempts, the interactive nature of pre-signing seems necessary for this notion of strong privacy.
An immediate challenge towards non-interactive pre-signing would be to build a simulation-secure IPFE scheme where the buyer can itself generate
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{f}\leftarrow{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf PubKGen}({\sf mpk},f)
such that the seller can generate the corresponding functional secret-key
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf KGen}({\sf msk},f)
.
Unfortunately, the randomized nature of the key-generation algorithm of simulation-secure scheme is a major hurdle to enabling this.

We explore whether a non-interactive pre-signing can be achieved for FAS for relaxed privacy definitions. Towards this, we study FAS that only satisfies
witness indistinguishability
.
The construction of such a FAS follows from our construction template by replacing the simulation-secure IPFE with an appropriate IND-secure IPFE.
To understand the intuition, we refer the reader to the IPFE compliance example discussed in our technique 1 in
Section

2.2
.
We provide more details on this construction
in
Appendix

B
.

3
Preliminaries

We recall the cryptographic tools needed in this work.

Notations.

We denote by
x\leftarrow S
the experiment of sampling
x
from a probability distribution
S
.
We denote by
[A(\cdot)]
the range of an algorithm
A(\cdot)
.
If
p(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes a predicate, then
\Pr[p(y,z):x\leftarrow S,(y,z)\leftarrow A(x)]
is the probability that the predicate
p(y,z)
is true after the ordered sequence of events
x\leftarrow S
followed by
(y,z)\leftarrow A(x)
.
We denote scalars by lower-case alphabets such as
x
,
vectors by bold-face lower-case alphabets such as
{\bf x}
,
matrices by bold-face upper-case alphabets such as
{\bf X}
.

{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2})^{T}
denotes a column-vector with elements
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
.

{\bf x}^{T}
denotes a row-vector.
Suppose
\mathbb{G}
is a cyclic group of prime-order
p
and with generator
g
.
For a scalar
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
,

g^{x}
denotes its group encoding.
For a vector
{\bf x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})^{T}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}
,

g^{\bf x}
denotes its group encoding
(g^{x_{1}},\ldots,g^{x_{n}})^{T}
.
Similarly, for a matrix
{\bf X}
,
g^{\bf X}
denotes its group encoding.
Given group encoding of a vector
g^{\bf x}
and a vector
{\bf y}
, we can compute
g^{{\bf x}^{T}{\bf y}}
efficiently as
g^{{\bf x}^{T}{\bf y}}=\Pi_{i\in[n]}(g^{x_{i}})^{y_{i}}
.

Linear Functions.

We denote linear functions as inner product computation.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
denote the family of inner products of vectors of length
\ell
.
For a prime
p
, in this work we study two restrictions of inner product computations as follows:

•

Inner products modulo
p
.

The function class
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell,p}=\{f_{\bf y}:{\bf y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}\}
, where
f_{\bf y}:\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is defined as
f_{\bf y}({\bf x})={\bf x}^{T}{\bf y}\bmod{p}
.

•

Inner products with output values polynomially bounded by
B\ll p
.

The function class
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell,p,B}=\{f_{\bf y}:{\bf y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}\}
, where
f_{\bf y}:\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}\to\{0,\ldots,B\}
is defined as
f_{\bf y}({\bf x})={\bf x}^{T}{\bf y}\in\{0,\ldots,B\}
.

Often we will use
{\bf y}
instead of
f_{\bf y}
to denote the function.

Digital Signature.

A digital signature scheme

\mathsf{DS}:=({\sf KGen},{{\sf Sign}},\allowbreak{{\sf Vf}})

has a key generation algorithm
(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow{\sf KGen}(1^{\lambda})

that outputs a verification-signing key pair.
Using signing key
\mathsf{sk}
we can compute signatures on a message
m

by running
\sigma\leftarrow{{\sf Sign}}(\mathsf{sk},m)
,
which can be publicly verified using the corresponding verification key
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk}

by running
{{\sf Vf}}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,\sigma)
.
We require the digital signature scheme to satisfy strong existential unforgeability
[
GMR88
]
.

3.1
Hard Relation

We recall the notion of a hard relation
R
with statement/witness pairs

(X,x)
. We denote by
L_{R}
the associated language defined as

L_{R}:=\{\ X\ |\ \exists\ x,(X,x)\in R\ \}
.

Definition 3.1

(Hardness of
R
)
.

We say that a relation
R
is hard, if
(i) there exists a
p.p.t.
sampling algorithm
\mathsf{GenR}(1^{\lambda})
that
outputs a statement/witness pair
(X,x)\in R
,
(ii) the relation is poly-time decidable,
(iii) for every non-uniform PPT adversary
{\cal A}
, there exists a negligible function
{\sf negl}
such that for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,

\Pr[\mathsf{G}_{{\cal A},R}(1^{\lambda})=1]\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda)\ ,

(1)

where the game
\mathsf{G}_{{\cal A},R}(1^{\lambda})
is defined as follows:

\got@maxcolwd

Game
\mathsf{G}_{{\cal A},R}(1^{\lambda})

\displaystyle\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 1}:\hskip 9.24994pt

(X,x)\leftarrow\mathsf{GenR}(1^{\lambda})

\displaystyle\@pc@lb\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 2}:\hskip 9.24994pt

(x^{\prime})\leftarrow{\cal A}(1^{\lambda},X)

\displaystyle\@pc@lb\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 3}:\hskip 9.24994pt

If
(X,x^{\prime})\in R
:
{\bf ret}

1

\displaystyle\@pc@lb\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 4}:\hskip 9.24994pt

{\bf ret}

0

Definition 3.2

(Hardness of
R
w.r.t. function class
\mathcal{F}
)
.

We say that a relation
R
is
\mathcal{F}
-hard, if
(i) there exists a
p.p.t.
sampling algorithm
\mathsf{GenR}(1^{\lambda})
that
outputs a statement/witness pair
(X,x)\in R
,
(ii) for every non-uniform PPT adversary
{\cal A}
there exists a negligible function
{\sf negl}
such that for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,

\Pr[\mathsf{G}_{{\cal A},R,\mathcal{F}}(1^{\lambda})=1]\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda)\ ,

(2)

where the game
\mathsf{G}_{{\cal A},R,\mathcal{F}}(1^{\lambda})
is defined as follows:

\got@maxcolwd

Game
\mathsf{G}_{{\cal A},R,\mathcal{F}}(1^{\lambda})

\displaystyle\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 1}:\hskip 9.24994pt

(X,x)\leftarrow\mathsf{GenR}(1^{\lambda})

\displaystyle\@pc@lb\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 2}:\hskip 9.24994pt

(f,z)\leftarrow{\cal A}(1^{\lambda},X)

\displaystyle\@pc@lb\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 3}:\hskip 9.24994pt

If
(f\in\mathcal{F})\wedge\left(z\in\{f(x^{\prime}):\exists\ x^{\prime}\text{ such that }(X,x^{\prime})\in R\}\right)
:
{\bf ret}

1

\displaystyle\@pc@lb\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 4}:\hskip 9.24994pt

{\bf ret}

0

We note that in the above definition we allow the adversary
{\cal A}
to choose
f
adaptively as this hardness assumption will later be used in proving unforgeability of our functional adaptor signatures construction where the adversary is allowed to choose the challenge function adaptively. This is necessary as functional adaptor signatures are non-trivial to build only in such a setting and become trivial if the challenge function is selectively chosen upfront by the adversary. Further note that we let the adversary
{\cal A}
win even if
{\cal A}
outputs a function evaluation of a witness
x^{\prime}
of
X
that is different from
x
.

Definition 3.3

(Hard Relation
R_{\mathsf{DL}}
)
.

The discrete log language
L_{\mathsf{DL}}
is defined with respect
to a group
G
with generator
g
and order
p
as

L_{\mathsf{DL}}:=\{\ X\ |\ \exists\ x\in\mathbb{Z}_{p},X=g^{x}\ \}

with the corresponding hard relation
R_{\mathsf{DL}}=\{\ (X,x)\ |\ X=g^{x}\ \}
.

3.2
Adaptor Signatures

Adaptor Signatures were first formally defined in
[
AEE+21
]
.

[
EEE20a
]
generalized the syntax in
[
AEE+21
]
to enable constructing adaptor signatures from lattices.
More concretely,
[
AEE+21
]
defined adaptor signatures w.r.t. a digital signature scheme and a hard relation
R
.

[
EEE20a
]
generalized it to be w.r.t. two hard relations
R
and
R^{\prime}
such that such that
R\subseteq R^{\prime}
. For the group-based constructions, typically we have
R=R^{\prime}
, but for the lattice based constructions, typically we have
R\neq R^{\prime}
.
In this work, one of our constructions will be from lattices, hence, we follow this generalized syntax.

In a different vein,
[
DOY22
,
GSST24
]
have strengthened the security properties of adaptor signatures, with notions like
unforgeability
,
pre-signature extractability
, and
witness extractability
.

[
DOY22
]
further added
unique extractability
and
unlinkability
as security properties and unified
unforgeability
and
witness extractability
under a single security property called
extractability
.
[
GSST24
]
further added
pre-verify soundness
as a security property. We note that not all security properties are needed always. Within the scope of this work, we will use adaptor signatures as a building block for the construction of FAS and we only need
pre-signature adaptability
and
witness extractability
security properties of adaptor signatures.
Intuitively, pre-signature adaptability ensures that
given a valid pre-signature and a witness for the statement, one can always adapt the pre-signature into a valid signature.
Witness extractability requires that given an honestly generated pre-signature and a valid signature, one should be able to extract a witness.
We define these formally below.

Definition 3.4
.

An adaptor signature scheme

\mathsf{AS}_{\mathsf{DS},R,R^{\prime}}:=(\mathsf{PreSign},

\mathsf{PreVerify},\mathsf{Adapt},\mathsf{Ext})

is defined with respect to a signature scheme
\mathsf{DS}=({\sf KGen},{{\sf Sign}},{{\sf Vf}})

and hard relations
R
and
R^{\prime}
such that
R\subseteq R^{\prime}
.
Here,
R
constitutes the relation for the statement-witness pairs generated by
\mathsf{GenR}
and
R^{\prime}
is an extended relation that defines the relation for extracted witnesses.
The interfaces are described below.

•

\widetilde{\sigma}\leftarrow\mathsf{PreSign}(\mathsf{sk},m,X)
:
The pre-signing algorithm takes as input
a signing key
\mathsf{sk}
,
a message
m
,
and a statement
X
for the language
L_{R}
,
and outputs a pre-signature
\widetilde{\sigma}

(we sometimes also refer to this as a partial signature).

•

0/1\leftarrow\mathsf{PreVerify}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,X,\widetilde{\sigma})
:
The pre-signature verification algorithm takes as input
a verification key
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk}
,
a message
m
,
a statement
X
for the language
L_{R}
,
and a pre-signature
\widetilde{\sigma}
,
and outputs
0/1
signifying whether
\widetilde{\sigma}

is correctly generated.

•

\sigma:=\mathsf{Adapt}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,X,x,\widetilde{\sigma})
:
The adapt algorithm transforms
a pre-signature
\widetilde{\sigma}
into a valid signature
\sigma

given the witness
x
for the instance
X
of the language
L_{R}
.

•

x:=\mathsf{Ext}(\widetilde{\sigma},\sigma,X)
:
The extract algorithm takes as input
a pre-signature
\widetilde{\sigma}
,
a signature
\sigma
,
and an instance
X
,
and outputs a witness
x^{\prime}
such that
(X,x^{\prime})\in R^{\prime}
, or
\bot
.

Note that an adaptor signature scheme
\mathsf{AS}_{\mathsf{DS},R,R^{\prime}}
also inherits
{\sf KGen},{{\sf Sign}},{{\sf Vf}}
algorithms from the signature scheme
\mathsf{DS}
.

Typically, an adaptor signature is run between a buyer and a seller.
The buyer runs
{\sf KGen},{{\sf Sign}},\allowbreak\mathsf{PreSign},\mathsf{Ext}
algorithms,
the seller runs
\mathsf{Adapt}
algorithm, and anyone can run
\mathsf{PreVerify},{{\sf Vf}}
algorithms.

Remark 3.5
.

Looking ahead, in the discrete-log based instantiation, we will have
R^{\prime}=R
, but in the lattice-based instantiation, we will have
R^{\prime}\neq R
and the reason for this extension is the knowledge/soundness gap inherent in efficient lattice-based zero-knowledge proofs.

Definition 3.6

(Correctness)
.

An adaptor signature scheme

\mathsf{AS}
satisfies correctness if for every

n\in\mathbb{N}
, every message
m\in\{0,1\}^{*}
,
and every statement/witness pair
(X,x)\in R
, the following holds:

\Pr\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{PreVerify}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,X,\widetilde{\sigma})=1\\
\wedge\ {{\sf Vf}}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,\sigma)=1\\
\wedge\ (X,x^{\prime})\in R^{\prime}\end{array}:\begin{array}[]{l}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk})\leftarrow{\sf KGen}(1^{\lambda})\\
\widetilde{\sigma}\leftarrow\mathsf{PreSign}(\mathsf{sk},m,X)\\
\sigma:=\mathsf{Adapt}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,X,x,\widetilde{\sigma})\\
x^{\prime}:=\mathsf{Ext}(\widetilde{\sigma},\sigma,X)\end{array}\right]=1

In terms of security, we want witness extractability against a malicious seller and weak pre-signature adaptability against a malicious seller. We explain these next.

Witness extractability requires that for an honestly generated pre-signature and a valid signature, one should be able to extract a witness. Intuitively, it tries to capture that an interaction between an honest buyer (i.e., generates valid pre-signature) and a malicious seller trying to get paid (i.e., adapt pre-signature into a valid signature) without revealing a witness (i.e., extraction failure) should be unlikely to occur.

Definition 3.7

(Witness Extractability)
.

An adaptor signature scheme
\mathsf{AS}
is witness extractable if for every PPT adversary
{\cal A}
, there exists a negligible function
{\sf negl}
such that
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,

\Pr[\mathsf{aWitExt}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{AS}}(1^{\lambda})=1]\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda),

where the experiment
\mathsf{aWitExt}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{AS}}
is defined as in
Figure

3
.

\got@maxcolwd

Experiment
\mathsf{aWitExt}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{AS}}
.

\displaystyle\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 1}:\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\vphantom{\rule[1.93748pt]{0.0pt}{0.0pt}}\mathcal{Q}:=\emptyset

\displaystyle(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk})\leftarrow{\sf KGen}(1^{\lambda})

\displaystyle(m,X)\leftarrow{\cal A}^{\mathcal{O}_{S}(\cdot),\mathcal{O}_{pS}(\cdot,\cdot)}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk})

\displaystyle\widetilde{\sigma}\leftarrow\mathsf{PreSign}(\mathsf{sk},m,X)

\displaystyle\sigma\leftarrow{\cal A}^{\mathcal{O}_{S}(\cdot),\mathcal{O}_{pS}(\cdot,\cdot)}(\widetilde{\sigma})

\displaystyle x^{\prime}:=\mathsf{Ext}(\widetilde{\sigma},\sigma,X)

\displaystyle\text{${\bf ret}$\ }((m\notin\mathcal{Q})\wedge((X,x^{\prime})\notin R^{\prime})\wedge{{\sf Vf}}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,\sigma))

\got@maxcolwd

Oracle
\mathcal{O}_{S}(m)

\displaystyle\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 1}:\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\vphantom{\rule[1.93748pt]{0.0pt}{0.0pt}}\sigma\leftarrow{{\sf Sign}}(\mathsf{sk},m)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}:=\mathcal{Q}\vee\{m\}

\displaystyle\text{${\bf ret}$\ }\sigma

\got@maxcolwd

Oracle
\mathcal{O}_{pS}(m,X)

\displaystyle\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 1}:\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\vphantom{\rule[1.93748pt]{0.0pt}{0.0pt}}\widetilde{\sigma}\leftarrow\mathsf{PreSign}(\mathsf{sk},m,X)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}:=\mathcal{Q}\vee\{m\}

\displaystyle\text{${\bf ret}$\ }\widetilde{\sigma}

Figure 3
:
Witness Extractability experiment of adaptor signatures

Note that, in the above witness extractability definition, the adversary’s winning condition is restricted to the extracted witness not being in
R^{\prime}
.
Since
R\subseteq R^{\prime}
,
(X,x^{\prime})\notin R^{\prime}
implies
(X,x^{\prime})\notin R
. Therefore, it is sufficient to ensure that
R^{\prime}
is a hard relation, which itself implies that
R
is also a hard relation. As a result, in our security assumptions, we make sure that
R^{\prime}
is a hard relation.

Weak pre-signature adaptability requires that given a valid pre-signature and a witness for the instance,
one can always adapt the pre-signature into a valid signature.
Intuitively, this tries to capture that it should be impossible for a malicious buyer to learn the witness without doing the payment.

Definition 3.8

(Weak Pre-signature Adaptability)
.

An adaptor signature scheme
\mathsf{AS}
satisfies weak pre-signature adaptability
if for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
any message
m\in\{0,1\}^{*}
,
any key pair
(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk})\leftarrow{\sf KGen}(1^{\lambda})
,
any statement/witness pair
(X,x)\in R
,
and any pre-signature
\widetilde{\sigma}\in\{0,1\}^{*}

with
\mathsf{PreVerify}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},\allowbreak m,X,\widetilde{\sigma})=1
, we have:

\Pr[{{\sf Vf}}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,\mathsf{Adapt}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,X,x,\widetilde{\sigma}))=1]=1.

Note that the above pre-signature adaptability is called
weak
because only statement-witness pairs satisfying
R
are guaranteed to be adaptable, and not those satisfying
R^{\prime}
. Therefore, weak pre-signature adaptability does not guarantee, for example, that an
extracted
witness can be used to adapt a pre-signature successfully. This is however guaranteed whenever
R=R^{\prime}
.

We have several constructions of adaptor signatures compatible with ECDSA
[
AEE+21
]
, lattice-based signatures
[
EEE20a
,
ACL+22
]
, and dichotomic signature schemes
[
GSST24
]
which is a recently introduced abstraction that captures many popular schemes like Schnorr, CL, BBS, etc. The group-based schemes are constructed w.r.t. the discrete logarithm NP language in the respective groups, while the lattice-based schemes are constructed w.r.t. the short integer solution NP language.

3.3
Non-Interactive Zero-Knowledge Arguments

Definition 3.9

(Secure NIZK Argument System)
.

A
{\mathsf{NIZK}}
for language
L_{R}
in the common reference string (CRS) model consists of the following possibly randomized algorithms.

•

{\sf crs}\leftarrow{\sf Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:
it takes in a security parameter
\lambda

and outputs the common reference string
{\sf crs}
which is publicly known to everyone.

•

\pi\leftarrow{{\sf Prove}}({\sf crs},\mathsf{stmt},\mathsf{wit})
:
it takes in the common reference string
{\sf crs}
,
a statement
\mathsf{stmt}
,
and a witness
\mathsf{wit}
,
outputs a proof
\pi
.

•

0/1\leftarrow{{\sf Vf}}({\sf crs},\mathsf{stmt},\pi)
:
it takes in the common reference string
{\sf crs}
,
a statement
\mathsf{stmt}
,
and a proof
\pi
,
and either accepts (with output
1
) the proof, or rejects (with output
0
) it.

A secure
{\mathsf{NIZK}}
argument system must satisfy the following properties:

•

Completeness:

For all
\mathsf{stmt},\mathsf{wit}
where
R(\mathsf{stmt},\mathsf{wit})=1
,
if
{\sf crs}\leftarrow{\sf Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and

\pi\leftarrow{{\sf Prove}}({\sf crs},\allowbreak\mathsf{stmt},\mathsf{wit})
, then,

\Pr[{{\sf Vf}}({\sf crs},\mathsf{stmt},\pi)=1]=1.

•

Adaptive Soundness:

For all non-uniform
p.p.t.
provers
\mathcal{P}^{*}
,
there exists a negligible function
{\sf negl}
such that
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
if
{\sf crs}\leftarrow{\sf Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and

(\mathsf{stmt},\pi)\leftarrow\mathcal{P}^{*}({\sf crs})
, then,

\Pr[\mathsf{stmt}\notin L_{R}\wedge{{\sf Vf}}({\sf crs},\mathsf{stmt},\pi)=1]\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda).

•

Zero-Knowledge:

There exists a

p.p.t.
simulator
{\mathsf{Sim}}=({\sf Setup}^{*},\allowbreak{{\sf Prove}}^{*})

and there exists a negligible function
{\sf negl}
such that
for all
p.p.t.
distinguishers
{\cal D}
,
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
for all
(\mathsf{stmt},\mathsf{wit})\in R
,

|\Pr[{\cal D}({\sf crs},\pi)=1]-\Pr[{\cal D}({\sf crs}^{*},\pi^{*})=1]|\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda),

where

{\sf crs}\leftarrow{\sf Setup}(1^{\lambda})
,

\pi\leftarrow{{\sf Prove}}({\sf crs},\mathsf{stmt},

\mathsf{wit})
,

({\sf crs}^{*},\mathsf{td})\allowbreak\leftarrow{\sf Setup}^{*}(1^{\lambda})
,

\pi^{*}\allowbreak\leftarrow{{\sf Prove}}^{*}({\sf crs}^{*},\mathsf{td},\allowbreak\mathsf{stmt})
and

\mathsf{td}
is a trapdoor used by
{\mathsf{Sim}}
to come up with proof
\pi^{*}
for a statement
\mathsf{stmt}
without knowing its witness.

NIZK arguments are known from
factoring
[
FLS90
]
,
and standard assumptions on
pairings
[
CHK03
,
GOS06
]
and
lattices
[
PS19
,
CCH+19
]
.

3.4
Inner Product Functional Encryption

We will need a inner-product functional encryption scheme (IPFE).
Let
\ell
be an integer.
We define IPFE for computing inner products of vectors of length
\ell
. Let the message space be
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
and function space
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
. Messages are denoted by
{\bf x}\in\mathcal{M}
, functions are denoted by
{\bf y}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
and the function evaluation is denoted by
f_{\bf y}({\bf x})
(all three of them are parameterized by the prime
p\in\mathsf{pp}
as output by
{\sf Gen}
below).
Formally, IPFE consists of the
following algorithms:

•

\mathsf{pp}\leftarrow{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda})
:
it is a
randomized
algorithm that
takes in a security parameter
\lambda

and samples public parameters
\mathsf{pp}
.
We assume that
\mathsf{pp}
contains a prime number
p
.

•

({\sf mpk},{\sf msk})\leftarrow{\sf Setup}(\mathsf{pp},\ell)
:
it is a
randomized
algorithm that
takes in the public parameters
\mathsf{pp}
and the vector length
\ell
,
outputs a master public key
{\sf mpk}
and
a master secret key
{\sf msk}
.

•

\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}:={\sf KGen}({\sf msk},{\bf y}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell})
:
it is a
deterministic
algorithm that
takes in the master secret key
{\sf msk}
, and
a vector
{\bf y}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
,
outputs a functional secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}
.

•

{\sf ct}\leftarrow{\sf Enc}({\sf mpk},{\bf x}\in\mathcal{M})
:
it is a
randomized
algorithm that
takes in the master public key
{\sf mpk}
,
a plaintext vector
{\bf x}\in\mathcal{M}
,
and outputs a ciphertext
{\sf ct}
.

•

v:={\sf Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y},{\sf ct})
:
it is a
deterministic
algorithm that
takes in the functional secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}

and a ciphertext
{\sf ct}
, and outputs a decrypted outcome
v
.

Further, we augment the IPFE scheme with an additional algorithm
{\sf PubKGen}
defined as follows.

•

\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}:={\sf PubKGen}({\sf mpk},{\bf y}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell})
:
it is a
deterministic
algorithm that
takes in the master public key
{\sf mpk}
, and
a vector
{\bf y}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
,
outputs a functional public key
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}
.

Next, we describe the correctness of IPFE.

Definition 3.10

(IPFE Correctness)
.

For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
\mathsf{pp}\leftarrow{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda})
,
then for any

\ell\in\mathbb{N},{\bf x}\in\mathcal{M},{\bf y}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
,
the following holds:

\Pr\left[v=f_{\bf y}({\bf x}):\begin{array}[]{l}({\sf mpk},{\sf msk})\leftarrow{\sf Setup}(\mathsf{pp},\ell),\\
\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}={\sf KGen}({\sf msk},{\bf y}),\\
{\sf ct}\leftarrow{\sf Enc}({\sf mpk},{\bf x}),\\
v={\sf Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y},{\sf ct})\end{array}\right]=1.

Next, we describe the security of IPFE.

Definition 3.11

(Selective, IND-security)
.

We say that the
{{\sf IPFE}}
scheme satisfies selective, IND-security,
iff for any non-uniform
p.p.t.
admissible adversary
{\cal A}
,
there exists a negligible function
{\sf negl}

such that for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N},\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

|\Pr[{\sf IPFE\text{-}IND\text{-}SEL}_{{\cal A},{{\sf IPFE}}}^{0}(1^{\lambda},\ell)=1]-\Pr[{\sf IPFE\text{-}IND\text{-}SEL}_{{\cal A},{{\sf IPFE}}}^{1}(1^{\lambda},\ell)=1]|\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda),

where experiments
{\sf IPFE\text{-}IND\text{-}SEL}_{{\cal A},{{\sf IPFE}}}^{b}(1^{\lambda})
for
b\in\{0,1\}
are described in
Figure

4
.
Here, an adversary
\mathcal{A}
is said to be admissible iff the following
holds with probability
1
:
for any
{\bf y}

submitted in a
\mathcal{O}_{\sf KGen}
oracle query,
it must be that

f_{\bf y}({\bf x}^{*}_{0})=f_{\bf y}({\bf x}^{*}_{1})
.

\got@maxcolwd

Experiment
{\sf IPFE\text{-}IND\text{-}SEL}_{{\cal A},{{\sf IPFE}}}^{b}(1^{\lambda},\ell)

\displaystyle\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 1}:\hskip 9.24994pt

\mathsf{pp}\leftarrow{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda})

\displaystyle\@pc@lb\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 2}:\hskip 9.24994pt

({\bf x}^{*}_{0},{\bf x}^{*}_{1})\leftarrow{\cal A}(\mathsf{pp})

\displaystyle\@pc@lb\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 3}:\hskip 9.24994pt

({\sf mpk},{\sf msk})\leftarrow{\sf Setup}(\mathsf{pp},\ell)
,

{\sf ct}^{*}\leftarrow{\sf Enc}({\sf mpk},{\bf x}^{*}_{b})

\displaystyle\@pc@lb\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 4}:\hskip 9.24994pt

b^{\prime}\leftarrow{\cal A}^{\mathcal{O}_{\sf KGen}(\cdot)}({\sf mpk},{\sf ct}^{*})

\displaystyle\@pc@lb\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 5}:\hskip 9.24994pt

{\bf ret}

b^{\prime}

\got@maxcolwd

Oracle
\mathcal{O}_{\sf KGen}({\bf y})

\displaystyle\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 1}:\hskip 9.24994pt

{\bf ret}

\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}={\sf KGen}({\sf msk},{\bf y})

Figure 4
:
Selective, IND-security experiment of Inner Product Functional Encryption

The above IND-security level is called selective because it requires the adversary to commit to the challenge message pair even before it sees
{\sf mpk}
. Stronger security models exist such as (i) semi-adaptive, IND-security where the adversary can commit to the challenge message pair after seeing
{\sf mpk}
but before gaining access to the oracle
\mathcal{O}_{\sf KGen}
and (ii) adaptive, IND-security where the adversary can commit to the challenge message pair after seeing
{\sf mpk}
and the power to make queries to the oracle
\mathcal{O}_{\sf KGen}
before and after committing to the challenge message pair. But, we only consider selective, IND-security as it suffices for the scope of this work.
We also note that any adaptive, IND-secure IPFE is also selective, IND-secure.

Remark 3.12
.

We note that
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}
does not enable decryption by any means, so it will not change the correctness requirement. It will also not affect security of IPFE in any way. To see this, note that
{\sf PubKGen}
is deterministic. So, an adversary can always compute
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}
on its own. Given that
{\sf KGen}
is also deterministic, one way to think of
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}
is as a statistically binding and computationally hiding commitment to
\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}
. Typically,
{\sf mpk}
also has this property w.r.t.
{\sf msk}
and it is always implicit in the security definitions of IPFE. We make this explicit below for
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}
and
\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}
because our functional adaptor signatures will rely on it.
We formalize this compliance requirement below.

Definition 3.13

(
R_{{\sf IPFE}}
-Compliant IPFE)
.

For a hard relation
R_{{\sf IPFE}}
, we say that an IPFE scheme is
R_{{\sf IPFE}}
compliant if
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
for any
\mathsf{pp}\leftarrow{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda})
,
for any
({\sf mpk},{\sf msk})\leftarrow{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Setup}(\mathsf{pp},1^{\ell})
,
for any
{\bf y}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
,
let
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}:={\sf PubKGen}({\sf mpk},{\bf y})
, and

\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}:={\sf KGen}({\sf msk},{\bf y})
.
Then, it must be the case that
(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y},\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y})\in R_{{\sf IPFE}}
.

Next, we describe an additional correctness property of IPFE that is tailored specifically to make IPFE compatible with adaptor signatures when the two are used in tandem in our functional adaptor signature construction. Looking ahead, we need this property because in our functional adaptor signature construction, we will use a standard adaptor signature to sell functional secret keys. Recall that adaptor signatures are defined w.r.t. two relations
R
and
R^{\prime}
. In this case,
R
will be
R_{{\sf IPFE}}
as defined in
Definition

3.13
and let us denote the corresponding extended relation
R^{\prime}
by
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
. Then, the
\mathsf{AS}.\mathsf{Ext}
algorithm of adaptor signature scheme
\mathsf{AS}
will extract a functional secret key
\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{\bf y}
as the witness for the adaptor statement
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}
. While
\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{\bf y}
will be a witness of
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
, it may not be a witness of the base relation
R_{{\sf IPFE}}
since
R_{{\sf IPFE}}\subseteq R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
, and we still want that
\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{\bf y}
should enable meaningful decryption.
We formalize this robustness requirement below.

Definition 3.14

(
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
-Robust IPFE)
.

Let
R_{{\sf IPFE}}
be the hard relation as defined in
Definition

3.13
.
Let
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
such that
R_{{\sf IPFE}}\subseteq R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
be an
an extended relation of
R_{{\sf IPFE}}
.
We say that an IPFE scheme is
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
robust if
IPFE correctness holds even w.r.t. a functional secret key satisfying
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
. More formally,
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
\mathsf{pp}\leftarrow{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda})
,
then for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

{\bf x}\in\mathcal{M},{\bf y}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
, the following holds:

\Pr\left[v=f_{\bf y}({\bf x}):\begin{array}[]{l}({\sf mpk},{\sf msk})\leftarrow{\sf Setup}(\mathsf{pp},\ell),\\
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}={\sf PubKGen}({\sf msk},{\bf y}),\\
\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{\bf y}\ s.t.\ (\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y},\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{\bf y})\in R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}},\\
{\sf ct}\leftarrow{\sf Enc}({\sf mpk},{\bf x}),\\
v={\sf Dec}(\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{\bf y},{\sf ct})\end{array}\right]=1.

Remark 3.15
.

One can think of decryption correctness (
Definition

3.10
) as being implicitly defined with respect to
\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}
that is witness to
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}
for the relation
R_{{\sf IPFE}}
. And one can think of the
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
-robustness as decryption correctness with respect to
\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{\bf y}
that is witness to
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}
for the relation
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
.

Abdalla et al.
[
ABDP15
]
constructed selective, IND-secure IPFE schemes from DDH and LWE. Subsequently, Agrawal et al.
[
ALS16
]
constructed adaptive, IND-secure IPFE schemes from DDH, DCR, and LWE.
Within the scope of this work, we will use the following two IPFE schemes:

•

The DDH based IPFE by Abdalla et al.
[
ABDP15
]
.
This scheme computes inner products with output values polynomially bounded by
B\ll p
.
Specifically, the message space is
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}
and the function class is
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell,p,B}=\{f_{\bf y}:{\bf y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}\}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
, where
f_{\bf y}:\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}\to\{0,\ldots,B\}
is defined as
f_{\bf y}({\bf x})={\bf x}^{T}{\bf y}\in\{0,\ldots,B\}
.

•

The LWE based IPFE by Agrawal et al.
[
ALS16
, Section 4.2]
.
This scheme computes inner products modulo
p
.
Specifically, the message space is
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}
and the function class is
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell,p}=\{f_{\bf y}:{\bf y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}\}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
, where
f_{\bf y}:\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is defined as
f_{\bf y}({\bf x})={\bf x}^{T}{\bf y}\bmod{p}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

3.5
Lattice Preliminaries

We denote by
{\cal S}_{c}=\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}:||{\bf x}||_{\infty}\leq c\}
the set of vectors in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
whose maximum absolute coefficient is at most
c\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.

Definition 3.16

(
{{\sf SIS}}_{n,m,q,\beta}
)
.

Let
{\bf A}{\overset{\$}{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
.
Given
{\bf A}
,
{{\sf SIS}}
problem with parameters
m>1
and
0<\beta<q
asks to find a short non-zero
{\bf v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
such that
{\bf A}{\bf v}=0\bmod{q}
and
||{\bf v}||_{\infty}\leq\beta
.

Next, we define
{{\sf SIS}}
in Hermite Normal Form (HNF).

Definition 3.17

(
{{\sf HNF\text{-}SIS}}_{n,m,q,\beta}
)
.

Let
{\bf A}^{\prime}{\overset{\$}{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times(m-1)}
and
{\bf A}=({\bf 1}||{{\bf A}^{\prime}})\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
.
Given
{\bf A}
,
{{\sf HNF\text{-}SIS}}
problem with parameters
m>1
and
0<\beta<q
asks to find a short non-zero
{\bf v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
such that
{\bf A}{\bf v}=0\bmod{q}
and
||{\bf v}||_{\infty}\leq\beta
.

Definition 3.18

(
{{\sf ISIS}}_{n,m,q,\beta}
)
.

It is the inhomogeneous version of
{{\sf HNF\text{-}SIS}}
.
Let
{\bf A}^{\prime}{\overset{\$}{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times(m-1)}
and
{\bf A}=({\bf 1}||{{\bf A}^{\prime}})\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
and
let
{\bf u}{\overset{\$}{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{Z}^{m}
s.t.
||{\bf u}||_{\infty}\leq\beta
.
Let
{\bf b}={\bf A}{\bf u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
.
Given
({\bf A},{\bf b})
, the problem with parameters
m>1
and
0<\beta<q
asks to find a short non-zero
{\bf v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
such that
{\bf A}{\bf v}={\bf b}\bmod{q}
and
||{\bf v}||_{\infty}\leq\beta
.

Definition 3.19

(
{{\sf LWE}}_{n,m,q,\Psi}
)
.

{{\sf LWE}}
problem with parameters
n,m>0
and distribution
\Psi
over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
asks to distinguish between the following two cases with non-negligible advantage: 1)
({\bf A},{\bf b}){\overset{\$}{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m\times n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
, and 2)
({\bf A},{\bf A}{\bf s}+{\bf e})
for
{\bf A}{\overset{\$}{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m\times n}
, a secret
{\bf s}{\overset{\$}{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and an error vector
{\bf e}\leftarrow\Psi^{m}
.

Definition 3.20

(Multi-hint extended-LWE)
.

Let
q,m,t
be integers,
\alpha
be a real and
\tau
be a distribution over
\mathbb{Z}^{t\times m}
, all of them functions of a parameter
n
. The multi-hint extended-LWE problem
{{\sf mheLWE}}_{n,m,q,\alpha,t,\tau}
is to distinguish between the distributions of the tuples

({\bf A},{\bf b},{\bf Z},{\bf Z}{\bf e})\text{ and }({\bf A},{\bf A}{\bf s}+{\bf e},{\bf Z},{\bf Z}{\bf e}),

where
{\bf A}{\overset{\$}{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m\times n}
,
{\bf s}{\overset{\$}{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
,
{\bf b}{\overset{\$}{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
,
{\bf e}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z},\alpha q}^{m}
,
{\bf Z}\leftarrow\tau
.

Lemma 3.21

(
[
ALS16
]
, Theorem 4)
.

Let
n\geq 100
,
q\geq 2
,
t<n
and
m
with
m=\Omega(n\log n)
and
m\leq n^{O(1)}
.
There exists
\xi\leq O(n^{4}m^{2}\log^{5/2}n)
and a distribution
\tau
over
\mathbb{Z}^{t\times m}
such that the following statements hold:

•

There is a reduction from
{{\sf LWE}}_{n-t,m,q,D_{\mathbb{Z},\alpha q}}
to
{{\sf mheLWE}}_{n,m,q,\alpha,t,\tau}
.

•

It is possible to sample from
\tau
in polynomial time in
n
.

•

Each entry of matrix
\tau
is an independent discrete Gaussian
\tau_{i,j}=D_{\mathbb{Z},\sigma_{i,j},c_{i,j}}
for some
c_{i,j}\in\{0,1\}
and
\sigma_{i,j}\geq\Omega(\sqrt{mn\log m})
.

•

With probability at least
1-n^{-\omega(1)}
, all rows from a sample from
\tau
have norms at most
\xi
.

Definition 3.22

(Discrete Gaussian distribution)
.

A discrete gaussian distribution
D_{\Lambda,\sqrt{\Sigma},{\bf c}}
, for
{\bf c}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\Sigma
a positive semi-definite matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, and a lattice
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, is a distribution with values in
\Lambda
and probabilities

\Pr[X={\bf x}]\propto\exp{\left(-\frac{1}{2}({\bf x}-{\bf c})^{T}\Sigma^{+}({\bf x}-{\bf c})\right)}.

Note that
\Sigma^{+}
denotes the pseudoinverse of a matrix. If
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, we shall just write
D_{\sqrt{\Sigma},{\bf c}}
. Further, if
{\bf c}=0
, then we shall just write
D_{\sqrt{\Sigma}}
, and if
\sqrt{\Sigma}=\sigma I_{n}
for
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
I_{n}
an identity matrix, we shall just write
D_{\sigma}
.

4
FAS Definition

A functional adaptor signature scheme shares similar syntax to an adaptor signature scheme with few additional interfaces and parameters.
Specifically,
the interfaces are
\mathsf{FAS}:=({\sf Setup},{\mathsf{AdGen}},{\mathsf{AdVerify}},\allowbreak{\mathsf{AuxGen}},{\mathsf{AuxVerify}},{\mathsf{FPreSign}},{\mathsf{FPreVerify}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Adapt},{\mathsf{FExt}})
.
We recall that
({\mathsf{AuxGen}},{\mathsf{AuxVerify}},\allowbreak{\mathsf{FPreSign}})
essentially denote the 3-round interactive pre-signing process. We describe the formal definition below.

Definition 4.1

(Functional adaptor signature)
.

A functional adaptor signature scheme

\mathsf{FAS}:=({\sf Setup},{\mathsf{AdGen}},{\mathsf{AdVerify}},{\mathsf{AuxGen}},

{\mathsf{AuxVerify}},{\mathsf{FPreSign}},{\mathsf{FPreVerify}},\mathsf{Adapt},{\mathsf{FExt}})

is defined with respect to a signature scheme
\mathsf{DS}=({\sf KGen},{{\sf Sign}},{{\sf Vf}})
, a hard relation
R
, and a family of functions
\mathcal{F}
. The interfaces are described below.

•

\mathsf{pp}\leftarrow{\sf Setup}(1^{\lambda})
: The setup algorithm takes as input the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
,
and outputs public parameters
\mathsf{pp}
.

•

(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{st})\leftarrow{\mathsf{AdGen}}(\mathsf{pp},X,x)
:
The advertisement generation algorithm takes as input
a public parameter
\mathsf{pp}
,
a statement
X
for the language
L_{R}
, and
a witness
x
, and
outputs a public advertisement
\mathsf{advt}
, and
a secret state
\mathsf{st}
.

•

0/1\leftarrow{\mathsf{AdVerify}}(\mathsf{pp},X,\mathsf{advt})
:
The advertisement verify algorithm takes as input
a public parameter
\mathsf{pp}
,
a statement
X
for the language
L_{R}
, and
a public advertisement
\mathsf{advt}
, and
outputs
0/1
signifying whether
\mathsf{advt}
is generated correctly.

•

({\sf aux}_{f},\pi_{f})\leftarrow{\mathsf{AuxGen}}(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{st},f)
:
The auxiliary value generation algorithm takes as input
a public advertisement
\mathsf{advt}
,
a secret state
\mathsf{st}
,
a function
f
belonging to the family
\mathcal{F}
, and
deterministically outputs an auxiliary value
{\sf aux}_{f}
, and
a proof
\pi_{f}
.

•

0/1\leftarrow{\mathsf{AuxVerify}}(\mathsf{advt},f,{\sf aux}_{f},\pi_{f})
:
The auxiliary value verify algorithm takes as input
a public advertisement
\mathsf{advt}
,
a function
f
belonging to the family
\mathcal{F}
,
an auxiliary value
{\sf aux}_{f}
, and
a proof
\pi_{f}
, and
outputs
0/1
signifying whether
{\sf aux}_{f}
is generated correctly.

•

\widetilde{\sigma}\leftarrow{\mathsf{FPreSign}}(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{sk},m,X,f,{\sf aux}_{f})
:
The functional pre-signing algorithm takes as input
a public advertisement
\mathsf{advt}
,
a signing key
\mathsf{sk}
,
a message
m
,
a statement
X
for the language
L_{R}
,
a function
f
belonging to the family
\mathcal{F}
, and
an auxiliary value
{\sf aux}_{f}
, and
outputs a pre-signature
\widetilde{\sigma}
.

•

0/1\leftarrow{\mathsf{FPreVerify}}(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,X,f,{\sf aux}_{f},\pi_{f},\widetilde{\sigma})
:
The pre-signature verification algorithm takes as input
a public advertisement
\mathsf{advt}
,
a secret state
\mathsf{st}
,
a verification key
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk}
,
a message
m
,
a statement
X
for the language
L_{R}
,
a function
f
belonging to the family
\mathcal{F}
,
an auxiliary value
{\sf aux}_{f}
,
a proof
\pi_{f}
,
and a pre-signature
\widetilde{\sigma}
,
and outputs
0/1
signifying whether
\widetilde{\sigma}

is correctly generated.

•

\sigma:=\mathsf{Adapt}(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{st},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,X,x,f,{\sf aux}_{f},\widetilde{\sigma})
:
The adapt algorithm transforms
a pre-signature
\widetilde{\sigma}
into a valid signature
\sigma

given the witness
x
for the instance
X
of the language
L_{R}
,
a function
f
belonging to the family
\mathcal{F}
,
an auxiliary value
{\sf aux}_{f}
,
a public advertisement
\mathsf{advt}
,
and a secret state
\mathsf{st}
.

•

z:={\mathsf{FExt}}(\mathsf{advt},\widetilde{\sigma},\sigma,X,f,{\sf aux}_{f})
:
The functional extract algorithm takes as input
a public advertisement
\mathsf{advt}
,
a pre-signature
\widetilde{\sigma}
,
a signature
\sigma
,
an instance
X
for the language
L_{R}
,
and a function
f\in\mathcal{F}
,
an auxiliary value
{\sf aux}_{f}
,
and outputs a value
z
in the range of
f
.

In a typical functional payment application, we will have a seller who holds a witness to the statement and wants to sell a function evaluation on it, and a buyer who wants to buy a function evaluation of the witness. We refer the reader to
Figure

1
for the protocol flow.

Definition 4.2

(Correctness)
.

A functional adaptor signature scheme

\mathsf{FAS}
satisfies correctness if for every

\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, every message
m\in\{0,1\}^{*}
,
every statement/witness pair
(X,x)\in R
,
every function
f\in\mathcal{F}
, suppose

\mathsf{pp}\leftarrow{\sf Setup}(1^{\lambda})
,

(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{st})\leftarrow{\mathsf{AdGen}}(\mathsf{pp},X,x)
,

(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk})\leftarrow{\sf KGen}(1^{\lambda})
,

({\sf aux}_{f},\pi_{f})\leftarrow{\mathsf{AuxGen}}(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{st},f)
,

\widetilde{\sigma}\leftarrow{\mathsf{FPreSign}}

(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{sk},m,X,f,{\sf aux}_{f})
,

\sigma:=\mathsf{Adapt}(

\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{st},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,X,x,f,\allowbreak{\sf aux}_{f},\widetilde{\sigma})
,
and
z:={\mathsf{FExt}}(\mathsf{advt},\widetilde{\sigma},\sigma,X,f,{\sf aux}_{f})
.
Then the following holds:

\Pr\left[\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathsf{AdVerify}}(\mathsf{pp},X,\mathsf{advt})=1\\
\wedge\ {\mathsf{AuxVerify}}(\mathsf{advt},f,{\sf aux}_{f},\pi_{f})=1\\
\wedge\ {\mathsf{FPreVerify}}(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,X,f,\pi_{f},\widetilde{\sigma})=1\\
\wedge\ {{\sf Vf}}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,\sigma)=1\ \wedge\ z=f(x)\par\end{array}\right]=1.

In terms of security, we want the following properties against malicous seller and buyer:

•

Malicious seller:

advertisement soundness,
pre-signature validity,
unforgeability,
witness extractability.

•

Malicious buyer:

pre-signature adaptability,
witness privacy.

Looking ahead, we will consider three notions of witness privacy: zero-knowledge, witness indistinguishability, and witness hiding. Among these, zero-knowledge is the strongest.
Based on this, we define two overall security levels of functional adaptor signatures below.

Definition 4.3

(Strongly-secure Functional Adaptor Signature Scheme)
.

A
\mathsf{FAS}
scheme is
strongly-secure
if it is
advertisement sound, pre-signature valid, unforgeable, witness extractable, pre-signature adaptable and zero-knowledge.

Definition 4.4

(Weakly-secure Functional Adaptor Signature Scheme)
.

A
\mathsf{FAS}
scheme is
weakly-secure
if it is
advertisement sound, pre-signature valid, unforgeable, witness extractable, pre-signature adaptable and witness indistinguishable.

We describe all the security properties next.

Advertisement Soundness.
The property requires that given
\mathsf{pp}
, it should be infeasible for a
p.p.t.
malicious seller to find statement
X\notin L_{R}
and advertisement
\mathsf{advt}
such that
\mathsf{advt}
is accepted by
{\mathsf{AdVerify}}
.

Definition 4.5

(Advertisement Soundness)
.

A functional adaptor signature scheme
\mathsf{FAS}
satisfies advertisement soundness if
for every
p.p.t.
adversary
{\cal A}
, there exists a negligible function
{\sf negl}
such that for all

\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, for all NP languages
L_{R}
,
\mathsf{pp}\leftarrow{\sf Setup}(1^{\lambda})
,
(X,\mathsf{advt})\leftarrow{\cal A}(\mathsf{pp})
,

\Pr[X\notin L_{R}\ \wedge\ {\mathsf{AdVerify}}(\mathsf{pp},X,\mathsf{advt})=1]\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda).

Pre-signature validity.

This property says that given a valid auxiliary value, one can always compute a valid pre-signature using it. Intuitively, this tries to capture that it should be impossible for a malicious seller to give out a malformed auxiliary value for the function
f
and yet learn an honestly generated pre-signature for
f
that is valid.

Definition 4.6

(Pre-signature Validity)
.

A functional adaptor signature scheme
\mathsf{FAS}
satisfies pre-signature validity if for
any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
any
\mathsf{pp}\leftarrow{\sf Setup}(1^{\lambda})
,
any
X,\mathsf{advt}
such that
{\mathsf{AdVerify}}(\mathsf{pp},\mathsf{advt},

X)=1
,
any message
m\in\{0,1\}^{*}
,
any function class
\mathcal{F}
,
any function
f\in\mathcal{F}
,
any
({\sf aux}_{f},\pi_{f})
,
any key pair
(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk})\leftarrow{\sf KGen}(1^{\lambda})
,
any pre-signature
\widetilde{\sigma}\leftarrow{\mathsf{FPreSign}}(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{sk},m,X,f,{\sf aux}_{f})
,
we have that if
{\mathsf{AuxVerify}}(\mathsf{advt},f,{\sf aux}_{f},\pi_{f})=1
, then,

\Pr[{\mathsf{FPreVerify}}(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,X,f,{\sf aux}_{f},\pi_{f},\widetilde{\sigma})=1]=1.

Unforgeability.

This is similar to the unforgeability security property typically defined for adaptor signatures
[
AEE+21
]
.
The property requires that even when the adversary (malicious seller) is given access to pre-signatures with respect to functions
f
of its choice, it should not be able to forge signatures if it does not know the witness
x^{*}
for the challenge statement
X^{*}
in the first place. To capture this essence, the experiment (See
Figure

5
) samples
(X^{*},x^{*})
at random. Beyond this, the adversary has all the powers of a (malicious) seller starting with generating
\mathsf{advt}
. A curious reader might ask if the adversary doesn’t know
x^{*}
, then, wouldn’t it typically fail to pass the
{\mathsf{AdVerify}}
check in step
5
in
Figure

5
and thus rendering the experiment after that pointless? The answer is no, because
advertisement soundness
is for statements not in the language
L_{R}
, but here
X^{*}\in L_{R}
as the experiment chooses it.

Witness extractability.

Here we require that for any function
f\in\mathcal{F}
, for any honestly generated pre-signature w.r.t.
f
and a valid signature, one should be able to extract the function evaluation
f
on a witness. Intuitively, it tries to capture the interaction between an honest buyer (i.e., generates valid pre-signature) and a malicious seller trying to get paid (i.e., adapt pre-signature into a valid signature) without revealing function evaluation
f
on a witness (i.e., extraction failure) should be unlikely to occur.
This is similar to the witness extractability security property of adaptor signatures (See
Definition

3.7
) except that in the formal witness extractability experiment
Figure

6
, in step
6
, computing the set
Z
is inefficient.

Witness Extractability of functional adaptor signatures

Pre-signature adaptability.

This property says that given a valid pre-signature and a witness for the instance,
one can always adapt the pre-signature into a valid signature.
Intuitively, this tries to capture that it should be impossible for a malicious buyer to learn a function evaluation on a witness without making the payment.
This is similar to the pre-signature adaptability security property of adaptor signatures (See
Definition

3.8
).

Definition 4.9

(Pre-signature Adaptability)
.

A functional adaptor signature scheme
\mathsf{FAS}
satisfies pre-signature adaptability
if for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
any
\mathsf{pp}\leftarrow{\sf Setup}(1^{\lambda})
,
any statement/witness pair
(X,x)\in R
,
any
(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{st})\leftarrow{\mathsf{AdGen}}(\mathsf{pp},X,x)
,
any message
m\in\{0,1\}^{*}
,
any function class
\mathcal{F}
,
any function
f\in\mathcal{F}
,
any
({\sf aux}_{f},\pi_{f})

\leftarrow{\mathsf{AuxGen}}(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{st},f)
,
any key pair
(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk})\leftarrow{\sf KGen}(1^{\lambda})
,
and any pre-signature
\widetilde{\sigma}\in\{0,1\}^{*}

with
{\mathsf{FPreVerify}}(\mathsf{advt},

\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,X,f,{\sf aux}_{f},\pi_{f},\allowbreak\widetilde{\sigma})=1
, we have:

\Pr[{{\sf Vf}}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,\mathsf{Adapt}(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{st},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,X,x,f,{\sf aux}_{f},\widetilde{\sigma}))=1]=1.

Witness privacy.

Here we want that in an interaction between a malicious buyer wanting to learn function evaluation
f
on the witness and an honest seller using witness
x
to adapt the pre-signature into a valid signature, at the end the buyer should not be able to extract any information about
x
beyond
f(x)
. There are different ways to capture this formally.
We consider various notions of witness privacy akin to those in functional encryption literature and zero-knowledge proofs literature as follows.

•

Zero-Knowledge:

for every
(X,x)\in R
, there exists a
p.p.t.
simulator
{\mathsf{Sim}}
such that the adversary should not be able to guess whether it is interacting with a real-world challenger that is allowed to use the witness
x
or the simulator
{\mathsf{Sim}}
that is only allowed access to
f(x)
.

•

Witness Indistinguishability:

given two valid witnesses
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
for a statement
X\in L_{R}
, the adversary should not be able to guess which witness was used to adapt a pre-signature as long as
f(x_{0})=f(x_{1})
. This notion is meaningful only for languages that have at least two witnesses.

•

Witness Hiding:

for every
X\in L_{R}
, given valid pre-signature w.r.t. function
f
and valid signature adapted from it, the adversary should not be able to guess a witness
x
s.t.
(X,x)\in R
.

Zero-Knowledge is the strongest among all in the sense that it implies the other two. All three are meaningful for non-unique witness languages. But for unique witness languages, witness indistinguishability is not meaningful.
Further, witness-indistinguishability and witness-hiding are incomparable.
Next, we describe all three formally.

Definition 4.10

(Zero-Knowledge)
.

A
\mathsf{FAS}
scheme satisfies zero-knowledge if
for every
p.p.t.
adversary
{\cal A}
,
there exists a stateful
p.p.t.
simulator
{\mathsf{Sim}}=({\sf Setup}^{*},{\mathsf{AdGen}}^{*},{\mathsf{AuxGen}}^{*},\mathsf{Adapt}^{*})
,
there exists a negligible function
{\sf negl}
s.t.
for all
p.p.t.
distinguishers
{\cal D}
,
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
for all
(X,x)\in R
,

|\Pr[{\cal D}(\mathsf{faZKReal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}(1^{\lambda},X,x))=1]-\Pr[{\cal D}(\mathsf{faZKIdeal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{\mathsf{Sim}}(1^{\lambda},X,x))=1]|\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda)
,
where experiments
\mathsf{faZKReal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}

5
Strongly-Secure FAS Construction

In this section, we build a generic construction of FAS w.r.t. digital signature scheme
\mathsf{DS}
, any NP relation
R
with statement/witness pairs
(X,{\bf x})\in R
such that
{\bf x}\in\mathcal{M}
for some set
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
, and function family
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
for computing inner products of vectors of length
\ell
.
Our generic construction of FAS is presented in
Figure

2

8
8

8

Figure

2
is presented with
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}
, but the scheme generalizes to
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
as well.

and it uses the following building blocks:

•

A selective, IND-secure
{{\sf IPFE}}
satisfying

R_{{\sf IPFE}}
-compliance and

R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
-robustness (
Definitions

3.11
,
3.13
and
3.14
)
w.r.t. hard relations
R_{{\sf IPFE}}
and
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
such that
R_{{\sf IPFE}}\subseteq R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
. The message space of
{{\sf IPFE}}
is
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{\ell+1}
and the function family is
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell+1}
.

•

An adaptor signature scheme
\mathsf{AS}
w.r.t. a digital signature scheme
\mathsf{DS}
, and hard relations
R_{{\sf IPFE}}
and
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
that satisfies witness
extractability (
Definition

3.7
)
and weak pre-signature
adaptability (
Definition

3.8
)
security properties.

•

A non-interactive zero-knowledge argument system
{\mathsf{NIZK}}
in the common reference string model for the NP language
L_{\mathsf{NIZK}}
:

L_{\mathsf{NIZK}}:=\left\{\begin{matrix}(X,\mathsf{pp}^{\prime},{\sf mpk},{\sf ct}):\\
\exists(r_{0},r_{1},{\bf x})\ \text{such that }\mathsf{pp}^{\prime}\in[{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda})],\\
({\sf mpk},{\sf msk})={{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Setup}(\mathsf{pp}^{\prime},1^{\ell+1};r_{0}),\\
(X,{\bf x})\in R,{\sf ct}={{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Enc}({\sf mpk},({\bf x}^{T},0)^{T};r_{1})\end{matrix}\right\}.

We sketch the security proof of unforgeability and zero-knowledge witness privacy below.
Correctness and formal proofs are deferred
to
Appendix

A
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Suppose NIZK satisfies correctness,
\mathsf{AS}
satisfies correctness, and
{{\sf IPFE}}
satisfies
R_{{\sf IPFE}}
-compliance and
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
-robustness. Then, the
\mathsf{FAS}
construction in
Figure

2
satisfies correctness.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
be the family of inner products functions of vectors of length
\ell
.
Let
R
be any NP relation
with statement/witness pairs
(X,{\bf x})
such that
{\bf x}\in\mathcal{M}
for some set
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
.
Suppose that

•

\mathcal{M}
is an additive group,

R
is

\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
-hard (
Definition

3.2
),

•

{\mathsf{NIZK}}
is a secure NIZK argument
system (
Definition

3.9
),

•

\mathsf{AS}
is an adaptor signature scheme w.r.t. digital signature scheme
\mathsf{DS}
and hard relations
R_{{\sf IPFE}},R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
satisfying weak pre-signature
adaptability (
Definition

3.8
),
witness
extractability (
Definition

3.7
),

•

{{\sf IPFE}}
is a selective, IND-secure IPFE
scheme (
Definition

3.11
)
for function family
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell+1}
satisfying
R_{{\sf IPFE}}
-compliance (
Definition

3.13
),
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
-robustness (
Definition

3.14
).

Then, the construction in
Figure

2
is a strongly-secure (
Definition

4.3
) functional adaptor signature scheme
w.r.t. digital signature scheme
\mathsf{DS}
, NP relation
R
, and family of inner product functions
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
.

Proof sketch of Unforgeability.

A natural idea for proving unforgeability of
\mathsf{FAS}
would be to somehow reduce it to unforgeability of
\mathsf{AS}
.
But, this requires non-blackbox use of the underlying
\mathsf{AS}
, and thus results in a complex proof.

We avoid that altogether and present a counter-intuitive yet elegant way of proving unforgeability of
\mathsf{FAS}
by reducing it to witness extractability of
\mathsf{AS}
. Our proof technique does not need to open up the blackbox of
\mathsf{AS}
as evident in the sequence of games below.

In a little more detail, to show that
\Pr[G_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda)
, we consider a sequence of games
G_{0},G_{1},G_{2}
as decribed in
Figure

10
.

\got@maxcolwd

G_{0}
,

\displaystyle G_{1}

,

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}G_{2}

Figure 10
:

Unforgeability Proof: Games
G_{0},G_{1},G_{2}

•

Game
G_{0}
is the original game
\mathsf{faEUF{\mathchar 45\relax}CMA}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}
, where the adversary
{\cal A}
has to come up with a valid forgery on a message
m^{*}
of his choice, while having access to functional pre-sign oracle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{fpS}}
and sign oracle
\mathcal{O}_{S}
. Here, variables
{\sf Bad}_{1},{\sf Bad}_{2}
do not affect the game and are used only to aide the analysis below.

•

Game
G_{1}
is same as
G_{0}
, except that
before computing the pre-signature,
the game checks if the NIZK statement
(X^{*},\mathsf{pp}^{\prime},{\sf mpk},{\sf ct})
is in the language
L_{NIZK}
.
If no, the game sets the flag
{\sf Bad}_{1}=\mathsf{true}
and returns
0
.
Observe that
G_{0}
and
G_{1}
are identical until
G_{1}
checks the condition for setting
{\sf Bad}_{1}
. Hence,

|\Pr[G_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]-\Pr[G_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1]|\leq\Pr[{\sf Bad}_{1}\text{ in $G_{1}$}].

Thus, for proving
|\Pr[G_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]-\Pr[G_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1]|\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda)
, it suffices to show that in game
G_{1}
,
\Pr[{\sf Bad}_{1}]\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda)
.

{\sf Bad}_{1}=\mathsf{true}
implies
\mathsf{stmt}\notin L_{NIZK}
and
{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{{\sf Vf}}({\sf crs},\mathsf{stmt},\pi)=1
. Thus, the probability bound follows from the adaptive soundness of NIZK argument system.

•

Game
G_{2}
is same as
G_{1}
, except that
when
{\cal A}
outputs the forgery
\sigma^{*}
,
the game extracts the witness
z
of the underlying adaptor signature scheme for the statement
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{{\bf y}^{*}}
and checks if the NIZK statement
({\sf aux}_{\bf y}^{*},z)
satisfies
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
.
If no, the game sets the flag
{\sf Bad}_{2}=\mathsf{true}
and returns
0
.
Observe that
G_{0}
and
G_{1}
are identical until
G_{1}
checks the condition for setting
{\sf Bad}_{1}
. Hence,

|\Pr[G_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1]-\Pr[G_{2}(1^{\lambda})=1]|\leq\Pr[{\sf Bad_{2}}\text{ in $G_{2}$}].

Thus, for proving
|\Pr[G_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1]-\Pr[G_{2}(1^{\lambda})=1]|\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda)
, it suffices to show that in game
G_{2}
,
\Pr[{\sf Bad}_{2}]\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda)
.
This follows from the witness extractability of the underlying adaptor signature scheme
\mathsf{AS}
.
In a little more detail, we can show that if an adversary
{\cal A}
successfully causes
G_{2}
to set
{\sf Bad}_{2}
, then, we can use it to come up with a reduction
{\cal B}
that breaks the witness extractability of the underlying adaptor signature scheme. This is because
{\sf Bad}_{2}=\mathsf{true}
implies
(m^{*}\notin\mathcal{Q})\wedge(({\sf aux}_{\bf y}^{*},z)\notin R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}})\wedge{{\sf Vf}}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m^{*},\sigma^{*})
, i.e., the winning condition for
{\cal B}
.

•

Lastly, we can show that
\Pr[G_{2}(1^{\lambda})=1]\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda)
assuming
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
-hardness of relation
R
and
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
-robustness of the
{{\sf IPFE}}
scheme.
Essentially, we show that if
{\cal A}
wins
G_{2}
, then, we can build a reduction
{\cal B}
that breaks the
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
-hardness of relation
R
.

{\cal B}
outputs
({\bf y}^{*},v)
, where
v={{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Dec}(z,{\sf ct})
.
To win,
{\cal B}
’s output must satisfy
({\bf y}^{*}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell})\wedge(v\in\{f_{{\bf y}^{*}}({\bf x}):\exists{\bf x}\ s.t.\ (X,{\bf x})\in R\})
.
If
{\cal A}
wins, then
{\bf y}^{*}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
and
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{{\bf y}^{*}}={\sf aux}_{\bf y}^{*}
.
Next,
{\sf Bad}_{1}=\mathsf{false}
implies that
\mathsf{stmt}\in L_{NIZK}
, where
\mathsf{stmt}=(X^{*},\mathsf{pp}^{\prime},{\sf mpk},{\sf ct})
. Hence, it follows that
{\sf ct}
encrypts some vector
\widetilde{{\bf x}}=({\bf x}^{T},0)^{T}\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{\ell+1}
under
{\sf mpk}
such that
(X^{*},{\bf x})\in R
.
Next,
{\sf Bad}_{2}=\mathsf{false}
implies that
({\sf aux}_{\bf y}^{*},z)\in R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
.
As
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{{\bf y}^{*}}={\sf aux}_{\bf y}^{*}
and IPFE satisfies
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
-robustness, it follows that
v=f_{\widetilde{{\bf y}^{*}}}(\widetilde{{\bf x}})
. As
\widetilde{{\bf y}^{*}}=({{\bf y}^{*}}^{T},\pi_{\bf y}^{*})^{T}
and
\widetilde{{\bf x}}=({\bf x}^{T},0)^{T}
, we get that
f_{\widetilde{{\bf y}^{*}}}(\widetilde{{\bf x}})=f_{{\bf y}^{*}}({\bf x})
.
Hence, we conclude that
v\in\{f_{{\bf y}^{*}}({\bf x}):\exists{\bf x}\ s.t.\ (X,{\bf x})\in R\}
. Thus,
{\cal B}
wins its game. This completes the proof.

Proof sketch of Zero-Knowledge.

We first describe the stateful simulator
{\mathsf{Sim}}=({\sf Setup}^{*},{\mathsf{AdGen}}^{*},\allowbreak{\mathsf{AuxGen}}^{*},\mathsf{Adapt}^{*})
.
Let the NIZK simulator be
{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}=({\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\sf Setup}^{*},{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{{\sf Prove}}^{*})
.
Then, the simulator
{\mathsf{Sim}}
is as follows.

•

{\sf Setup}^{*}(1^{\lambda})
: same as
{\sf Setup}
except
({\sf crs},\mathsf{td})\leftarrow{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\sf Setup}^{*}(1^{\lambda})
and trapdoor
\mathsf{td}
is stored as internal state by
{\mathsf{Sim}}
.

•

{\mathsf{AdGen}}^{*}(\mathsf{pp},X)
: same as
{\mathsf{AdGen}}
except
\widetilde{{\bf x}}:=(-{\bf t}^{T},1)^{T}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell+1}
and
\pi\leftarrow{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{{\sf Prove}}^{*}({\sf crs},\mathsf{td},(X,\mathsf{pp}^{\prime},\allowbreak{\sf mpk},{\sf ct}))
.

•

{\mathsf{AuxGen}}^{*}(\mathsf{advt},{\bf y},f_{{\bf y}}({\bf x}))
: same as
{\mathsf{AuxGen}}
except
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}
is computed for
\widetilde{{\bf y}}:=({\bf y}^{T},f_{\bf y}({\bf t})+f_{\bf y}({\bf x}))^{T}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell+1}
and thus
\pi_{\bf y}:=f_{\bf y}({\bf t})+f_{\bf y}({\bf x})
as well.

•

\mathsf{Adapt}^{*}(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,X,{\bf y},{\sf aux}_{\bf y},\widetilde{\sigma},f_{\bf y}({\bf x}))
: same as
\mathsf{Adapt}
except
\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}
for
\widetilde{{\bf y}}:=({\bf y}^{T},f_{\bf y}({\bf t})+f_{\bf y}({\bf x}))^{T}
is used.

To see that adversary’s views in real and ideal world (
Figure

7
) are indistinguishable, consider a sequence of games
G_{0},G_{1},G_{2},G_{3}
.

•

Game
G_{0}
corresponds to the real-world experiment where
{\sf Setup}
,
{\mathsf{AdGen}}
,
{\mathsf{AuxGen}}
,
\mathsf{Adapt}
are used.

•

Game
G_{1}
is same as
G_{0}
, except the NIZK is switched to simulation mode, i.e., we change
{\sf Setup}
to perform
({\sf crs},\mathsf{td})\leftarrow{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\sf Setup}^{*}(\allowbreak 1^{\lambda})
and change
{\mathsf{AdGen}}
to perform
\pi\leftarrow{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{{\sf Prove}}^{*}({\sf crs},\mathsf{td},(X,\allowbreak\mathsf{pp}^{\prime},\allowbreak{\sf mpk},{\sf ct}))
. It follows from the zero-knowledge property of NIZK that
G_{0}\approx_{c}G_{1}
.

•

Game
G_{2}
is same as
G_{1}
except
\widetilde{{\bf y}}
used in
{\mathsf{AuxGen}}
and
\mathsf{Adapt}
is switched from
\widetilde{{\bf y}}:=({\bf y}^{T},f_{\bf y}({\bf t}))^{T}
to
\widetilde{{\bf y}}:=({\bf y}^{T},f_{\bf y}({\bf t})+f_{\bf y}({\bf x}))^{T}
. Since
f
is a linear function, it follows that
f_{\bf y}({\bf t})+f_{\bf y}({\bf x})=f_{\bf y}({\bf t}+{\bf x})
, thus, one can view the transition to
G_{2}
as a change of variables
{\bf t}\to{\bf t}+{\bf x}
. Since
{\bf t}
is uniform random, it follows that
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are identically distributed.

•

Game
G_{3}
is same as
G_{2}
except that in
{\mathsf{AdGen}}
,
{\sf ct}
encrypts

\widetilde{{\bf x}}:=(-{\bf t}^{T},1)^{T}

instead of

\widetilde{{\bf x}}:=({\bf x}^{T},0)^{T}
.
Observe that this does not change the inner product value
\widetilde{{\bf x}}^{T}\widetilde{{\bf y}}
. Thus,
G_{2}\approx_{c}G_{3}
follows from IND-security of the IPFE scheme.

•

Lastly observe that adversary’s view in
G_{3}
is same as that in the ideal-world experiment. This complete the proof.

We emphasize that in
G_{3}
, even the underlying IPFE only uses the information
f_{\bf y}({\bf x})
about
{\bf x}
. Thus, going from
G_{1}
to
G_{3}
is essentially building an IND-security to simulation-security IPFE compiler. The random coins of this compiler are
{\bf t}
. Crucially,
G_{2}
and
G_{3}
explicitly use
f_{\bf y}({\bf t})
, which is a leakage on these random coins and for this reason the IPFE compiler is used in a non-blackbox way. We refer the reader back to
Remark

2.1
on why
f_{\bf y}({\bf t})
is an acceptable leakage on the IPFE simulator’s random coins
{\bf t}
.

6
FAS from Groups of Prime-Order

We provide instantiations of the FAS construction in
Figure

2
from prime order groups.
For this, it suffices to instantiate the building blocks
{{\sf IPFE}}
and
\mathsf{AS}
from prime order groups, while ensuring the two are compatible with each other. We describe these next.
We can instantiate the
{\mathsf{NIZK}}
for the language
L_{\mathsf{NIZK}}
depending on the concrete relation
R
.
That is, given
R
and the above instantiation of
{{\sf IPFE}}
, we can pick the most efficient
{\mathsf{NIZK}}
for
L_{\mathsf{NIZK}}
.
Since this is not the main contribution of this work, we will assume
R
to be a general NP relation and rely on the existence of
{\mathsf{NIZK}}
for general NP
[
JJ21
,
PS19
]
and not delve into its details here.

More concretely, we instantiate the
\mathsf{AS}
as Schnorr adaptor signature scheme
[
AEE+21
]
and thus, we have
R_{{\sf IPFE}}=R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}=R_{\mathsf{DL}}
as defined
in
Definition

3.3
.
Further, we show that the selective, IND-secure IPFE scheme by Abdalla et al.
[
ABDP15
]
satisfies
R_{\mathsf{DL}}
-compliance and
R_{\mathsf{DL}}
-robustness when appropriately augmented with a
{\sf PubKGen}
algorithm.

6.1
Schnorr Adaptor Signature

First, we recall the Schnorr Signature Scheme
\mathsf{Sch}

[
Sch90
]
.
Suppose
\mathsf{pp}:=(\mathbb{G},p,g)\leftarrow{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda})
as described in
Figure

11
, where
\mathbb{G}
is a cyclic group of prime order
p
and
g
is a generator of
\mathbb{G}
. Suppose
H:\{0,1\}^{*}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is a hash function modeled as a random oracle. Then
\mathsf{Sch}
is described below:

•

(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sch}.{\sf KGen}(\mathsf{pp})
: choose
\delta\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and set
\mathsf{sk}:=\delta
and
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk}:=g^{\delta}
.

•

\sigma\leftarrow\mathsf{Sch}.{{\sf Sign}}(\mathsf{sk},m)
: sample a randomness
r\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
to compute
h:=H(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk}||g^{r}||m),s:=r+h\delta
and output
\sigma:=(h,s)
.

•

0/1\leftarrow\mathsf{Sch}.{{\sf Vf}}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,\sigma)
: parse
\sigma:=(h,s)
and then compute
R:=g^{s}/\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk}^{h}
and if
h=H(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk}||R||m)
output
1
, otherwise output
0
.

Next, we describe the Adaptor Signature scheme
\mathsf{AS}
with respect to the digital signature scheme
\mathsf{Sch}
and hard relations
R=R^{\prime}=R_{\mathsf{DL}}
, where
R_{\mathsf{DL}}
is the discrete log relation. Let
L_{\mathsf{DL}}
be the corresponding language. Then,
\mathsf{AS}
is described below:

•

\widetilde{\sigma}\leftarrow\mathsf{AS}.\mathsf{PreSign}(\mathsf{sk},m,X\in L_{\mathsf{DL}})
: choose
r{\overset{\$}{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and compute
h:=H(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk}||g^{r}\cdot X||m)
and
\widetilde{s}:=r+h\delta
. Set
\widetilde{\sigma}:=(h,\widetilde{s})
.

•

0/1\leftarrow\mathsf{AS}.\mathsf{PreVerify}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,X,\widetilde{\sigma})
: parse
\widetilde{\sigma}:=(h,\widetilde{s})
and compute
R:=g^{s}/\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk}^{h}
. If
h=H(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk}||R\cdot X||m)

{\bf ret}

1
, else
{\bf ret}

0
.

•

\sigma\leftarrow\mathsf{AS}.\mathsf{Adapt}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,X,x,\widetilde{\sigma})
: parse
\widetilde{\sigma}=(h,\widetilde{s})
and compute
s:=\widetilde{s}+x
.
{\bf ret}

\sigma:=(h,s)

•

x^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{AS}.\mathsf{Ext}(\widetilde{\sigma},\sigma,X)
: parse
\widetilde{\sigma}=(h,\widetilde{s})
and
\sigma=(h,s)
. Compute
x^{\prime}:=s-\widetilde{s}
and if
(X,x^{\prime})\notin R_{\mathsf{DL}}
,
{\bf ret}

\bot
, else
{\bf ret}

x^{\prime}
.

Lemma 6.1

(
[
AEE+21
]
)
.

The adaptor signature scheme in
Section

6.1
satisfies witness extractability (
Definition

3.7
) and weak pre-signature adaptability (
Definition

3.8
).

6.2
IPFE from Groups of Prime-Order

We first recall the IPFE scheme by Abdalla et al.
[
ABDP15
]
. Then, we show that it satisfies the additional compliance and robustness properties needed by our FAS scheme.

Suppose
p
is a
\lambda
-bit prime number and suppose we want to compute inner products of vectors of length
\ell
.
The Abdalla et al.
[
ABDP15
]
IPFE scheme computes inner products with output values polynomially bounded by
B\ll p
.
Specifically, the message space is
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}
and the function class is
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell,p,B}=\{f_{\bf y}:{\bf y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}\}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
, where
f_{\bf y}:\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}\to\{0,\ldots,B\}
is defined as
f_{\bf y}({\bf x})={\bf x}^{T}{\bf y}\in\{0,\ldots,B\}
.
The scheme by Abdalla et al.
[
ABDP15
]
augmented with
{\sf PubKGen}
algorithm is as in
Figure

11
.
For
d\in\mathbb{G}
, we denote the algorithm for computing the discrete log of
d
with respect to base
g\in\mathbb{G}
by
\mathsf{DLog}_{g}(d)
.
As long as we are promised that the discrete log is bounded by a polynomial
B
, the
\mathsf{DLog}
algorithm simply enumerates all the possibilities to find the discrete log. The running time is polynomial because of the bound
B
.

\got@maxcolwd

{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda})

\displaystyle\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 1}:\hskip 9.24994pt

\mathsf{pp}:=(\mathbb{G},p,g)\leftarrow\mathsf{GGen}(1^{\lambda})

\displaystyle\@pc@lb\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 2}:\hskip 9.24994pt

{\bf ret}

\mathsf{pp}

\got@maxcolwd

{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Setup}(\mathsf{pp},1^{\ell})
