Lemma 6.2

(
[
ABDP15
]
)
.

Suppose the DDH assumption holds.
Then, the IPFE scheme in
Figure

11
is selective,
IND-secure (
Definition

3.11
).

Next, we show the IPFE scheme augmented with the above
{\sf PubKGen}
algorithm satisfies
R_{\mathsf{DL}}
-compliance and
R_{\mathsf{DL}}
-robustness.

Lemma 6.3
.

The IPFE scheme in
Figure

11
is
R_{\mathsf{DL}}
-compliant.

Proof.

For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
for any
\mathsf{pp}\leftarrow{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda})
as implemented in
Figure

11
,
for any
({\sf mpk},{\sf msk})\leftarrow{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Setup}(\mathsf{pp},1^{\ell})
as implemented in
Figure

11
,
for any
{\bf y}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell,p,B}
,
let
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}:={\sf PubKGen}(\allowbreak{\sf mpk},{\bf y})
as implemented in
Figure

11
, and

\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}:={\sf KGen}({\sf msk},{\bf y})
as implemented in
Figure

11
. Suppose here
{\sf msk}={\bf s}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}
. Then,
\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}={\bf s}^{T}{\bf y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}=g^{{\bf s}^{T}{\bf y}}\in\mathbb{G}
. Thus, clearly,
(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y},\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y})\in R_{\mathsf{DL}}
.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

The IPFE scheme in
Figure

11
is
R_{\mathsf{DL}}
-robust.

Proof.

Observe that in the IPFE scheme in
Figure

11
, the base relation
R
and the extended relation
R^{\prime}
are the same:
R=R^{\prime}=R_{\mathsf{DL}}
. Further,
R_{\mathsf{DL}}
is a unique witness relation. Thus,
R_{\mathsf{DL}}
-robustness follows trivially from the correctness of the IPFE scheme.
∎

6.3
\mathsf{FAS}
Construction

Instantiating
\mathsf{FAS}
construction in
Section

5
with
{{\sf IPFE}}
from
Figure

11
and
\mathsf{AS}
from
Section

6.1
, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 6.5
.

Let
p
be a
\lambda
-bit prime number and let
\ell
be an integer.
Let
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}
be an additive group.
let
\mathcal{F}_{\sf{IP},\ell,p,B}
be the function family for computing inner products of vectors in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}
such that the output value is bounded by some polynomial
B\ll p
.
Let
R
be any NP relation
with statement/witness pairs
(X,{\bf x})
such that
{\bf x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}
.
Suppose that

•

R
is
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell,p,B}
-hard (
Definition

3.2
),

•

{\mathsf{NIZK}}
is a secure NIZK argument system (
Definition

3.9
),

•

\mathsf{AS}
construction in
Section

6.1
is an adaptor signature scheme w.r.t. digital signature scheme
\mathsf{Sch}
and hard relation
R_{\mathsf{DL}}
that satisfies weak pre-signature adaptability and witness extractability (
Lemma

6.1
),

•

{{\sf IPFE}}
construction in
Figure

11
is a selective, IND-secure IPFE scheme (
Lemma

6.2
) for function family
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell+1}
that is
R_{{\sf IPFE}}
-compliant (
Lemma

6.3
) and
R_{{\sf IPFE}}
-robust (
Lemma

6.4
).

Then, the functional adaptor signature scheme
w.r.t. Schnorr signature scheme
\mathsf{Sch}
, NP relation
R
, and family of inner product functions
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell,p,B}

constructed in
Figure

2
is strongly-secure (
Definition

4.3
).

The proof of the above corollary is immediate from the proof of the
Theorem

5.2
concerning the generic construction, and
Lemmas

6.2
,
6.3
and
6.4
that show that the
{{\sf IPFE}}
scheme in
Figure

11
has the required properties.

7
\mathsf{FAS}
from lattices

In this section, we provide instantiations of the FAS construction in
Figure

2
from lattices.
For this, it suffices to instantiate the building blocks IPFE and AS from lattices, while ensuring the two are compatible with each other. We describe these next.

More specifically, we instantiate the
\mathsf{AS}
as the lattice-based adaptor signature scheme by Esgin et al.
[
EEE20a
]
. This scheme was built using cyclotomic ring
\mathcal{R}=\mathbb{Z}[x]/(x^{d}+1)
of degree
d=256
under Module-SIS and Module-LWE assumptions. Here, we set the degree to be
d=1
, thus giving us the ring of integers
\mathcal{R}=\mathbb{Z}
. Consequently, security follows from plain SIS and LWE assumptions. One can look at the resulting
\mathsf{AS}
to be w.r.t. a digital scheme
\mathsf{Lyu^{\prime}}
that is somewhere in between Lyubashevsky’s signature scheme
[
Lyu12
]
and Dilithium
[
DKL+18
]
instantiated with unstructured lattices.
Further,
\mathsf{AS}
is w.r.t. hard relations
R_{{\sf ISIS}}
and
R^{\prime}_{{\sf ISIS}}
such that
R_{{\sf ISIS}}\subset R^{\prime}_{{\sf ISIS}}
.

Further, we show that the IND-secure IPFE scheme by Agrawal et al.
[
ALS16
, Section 4.2]
satisfies
R_{{\sf ISIS}}
-compliance and
R^{\prime}_{{\sf ISIS}}
-robustness when appropriately augmented with a
{\sf PubKGen}
algorithm.
We describe the instantiations of these two building blocks in the subsequent sections.

7.1
Lattice-based Adaptor Signature

First, we recall the lattice-based signature scheme
\mathsf{Lyu^{\prime}}
, which can be seen as a variant of Lyubashevsky’s signature scheme
[
Lyu12
]
where the vector
{\bf y}
sampled during signing comes from a uniform distribution over a small range instead of discrete gaussian distribution.
It can also be seen as a variant of Dilithium signature scheme
[
DKL+18
]
instantiated over unstructured lattices.

Suppose
\mathsf{pp}:=(n,m,p,q,k,\alpha)\leftarrow{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda},p)
as described in
Figure

14
, where
p
is a prime number,
n=\lambda
,
q=p^{k}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, real
\alpha\in(0,1)
are chosen as described in the “Parameter choices” in
Section

7.2
.
Let
\mathcal{H}:\{0,1\}^{*}\to{\cal C}
be a family of hash functions (modelled as a random oracle), where
{\cal C}=\{{\bf c}\in\mathbb{Z}^{h}:||{\bf c}||_{1}=\kappa\wedge||{\bf c}||_{\infty}=1\}
.

Let
\gamma
be the maximum absolute coefficient of the masking randomness
{\bf y}
used in the
{{\sf Sign}}
algorithm below.
Then, the digital signature scheme
\mathsf{Lyu^{\prime}}
is as in
Figure

.

Next, we describe the Adaptor Signature scheme
\mathsf{AS}
with respect to the digital signature scheme
\mathsf{Lyu^{\prime}}
and hard relations

and

such that
R_{{\sf ISIS}}\subseteq R^{\prime}_{{\sf ISIS}}
.
Here,
\beta_{0}:=\ell pB_{\tau}
and
\beta_{1}:=2(\gamma-\kappa)-\beta_{0}
such that
\beta_{1}>\beta_{0}
and
\gamma-\kappa-\beta_{0}>0
.
Let
L_{{\sf ISIS}}
be the language corresponding to
R_{{\sf ISIS}}
and
R^{\prime}_{{\sf ISIS}}
.
Notice that
L_{{\sf ISIS}}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
.
Hence, for
R_{{\sf ISIS}}
, statements are of the form
({\bf A},X)\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times(n+m)}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
(same for
{}^{\prime}_{{\sf ISIS}}
) and witnesses are of the form
{\bf x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n+m}
such that
||{\bf x}||_{\infty}\leq\beta_{0}
(
||{\bf x}||_{\infty}\leq\beta_{1}
for
R^{\prime}_{{\sf ISIS}}
).
As
{\bf A}
is also part of the verification key, henceforth we drop it from the statement.

.

Parameter choices and comparison.

Our construction can be seen as similar to
[
EEE20a
]
but with degree
1
instead of
256
.

Our construction is different from
[
EEE20a
]
in the following aspects though:
[
EEE20a
]
chooses
\beta_{0}=1
, whereas we choose
\beta_{0}=\ell pB_{\tau}
, where parameters
\ell,p,B_{\tau}
are chosen based on the specification in the IPFE construction in
Figure

14
. Further,
[
EEE20a
]
sets
\beta_{1}=2(\gamma-\kappa)
whereas we set it a stricter bound on it
\beta_{1}=2(\gamma-\kappa)-\beta_{0}
as it is sufficient for correctness and security. Further, note that for the choice of
\beta_{1}
, the two constraints

\beta_{1}>\beta_{0}
and
\gamma-\kappa-\beta_{0}>0
are equivalent. Lastly, we note that just like in
[
EEE20a
]
, we can set
\gamma=2\kappa
to ensure that the average number of restarts in
{{\sf Sign}}
and
\mathsf{PreSign}
is about
e<3
, where
\kappa
is the
\ell_{1}
norm of values in the range set
{\cal C}
of the hash family
\mathcal{H}
.
Lastly, in
[
EEE20a
]
, the hash outputs belong to some polynomial ring
\mathcal{R}_{q}
, whereas we choose the range of hash outputs to be
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{h}
for some integer
h
.

Lemma 7.1

(
[
EEE20a
]
)
.

The adaptor signature scheme in
Figure

13
satisfies witness extractability (
Definition

3.7
) and weak pre-signature adaptability (
Definition

3.8
).

7.2
IPFE from Lattices

We first recall the IPFE scheme by Agrawal et al.
[
ALS16
]
. Then, we show that it satisfies the additional compliance and robustness properties needed by our FAS construction.

Suppose
p
is a
prime number and suppose we want to compute inner products of vectors of length
\ell
.
Let the set of messages be
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}
and
the function class be
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell,p}=\{f_{\bf y}:{\bf y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}\}
, where
f_{\bf y}:\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is defined as
f_{\bf y}({\bf x})={\bf x}^{T}{\bf y}\bmod{p}
.
Our IPFE scheme augmented with
{\sf PubKGen}
algorithm is as in
Figure

14
.

Remark 7.2
.

We note that the original construction needs
{\sf KGen}
to be stateful in order to support key generation queries that are linearly dependent modulo
p
. This is problematic for us as the corresponding
{\sf PubKGen}
will also have to be stateful then, but that defeats the purpose of this algorithm being public. So, in order to remedy the situation, we assume that all
{\sf KGen}
queries to
{{\sf IPFE}}
are going to be linearly independent.
Naively instantiating our
\mathsf{FAS}
construction (
Figure

2
) will result in
\mathsf{FAS}
only supporting linearly independent functions, which is acceptable if it involves a single buyer but in the most general case we would want to support multiple buyers who are agnostic of each other. Specifically, a buyer may engage in
\mathsf{FAS}
protocol to learn a function evaluation of the secret that is linearly dependent on something learnt by other some other buyers. To handle such a scenario, we show
in
Section

7.4

how to assign unique IDs to each buyer and make the underlying
{{\sf IPFE}}
key generation queries linearly independent.

\got@maxcolwd

{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda},p)

\displaystyle\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 1}:\hskip 9.24994pt

Let
n=\lambda
,
integers
m,k
, real
\alpha\in(0,1)
as defined below

\displaystyle\@pc@lb\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 2}:\hskip 9.24994pt

Let
q=p^{k}
,

{\bf ret}

\mathsf{pp}=(n,m,p,q,k,\alpha)

\got@maxcolwd

{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Setup}(\mathsf{pp},1^{\ell})

\displaystyle\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 1}:\hskip 9.24994pt

Sample
{\bf A}{\overset{\$}{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m\times n}
,
{\bf Z}\leftarrow\tau
where

distribution
\tau
over
\mathbb{Z}^{\ell\times m}
is as defined below

\displaystyle\@pc@lb\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 2}:\hskip 9.24994pt

Let
{\bf U}:={\bf Z}{\bf A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{\ell\times n}
,

{\bf ret}

{\sf mpk}:=({\bf A},{\bf U}),{\sf msk}:={\bf Z}

\displaystyle\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 1}:\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\@pc@lb\hskip 3.87498pt\text{\scriptsize 2}:\hskip 9.24994pt

v\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
that minimizes
\mid p^{k-1}v-d\mid

Figure 14
:

Agrawal et al.
[
ALS16
]
IPFE scheme augmented with
{\sf PubKGen}
algorithm. Note that
{\sf KGen}
here is stateless.

Parameter choices.

Let
B_{\tau}
be such that with probability at most
n^{-\omega(1)}
, each row of sample from
\tau
has
\ell_{2}
-norm at least
B_{\tau}
. Then, the parameter constraints for correctness and security are as follows.

•

\alpha^{-1}\geq\ell^{2}p^{3}B_{\tau}\omega(\sqrt{\log n})
,

q\geq\alpha^{-1}\omega(\sqrt{\log n})
,

•

\tau=D^{\ell\times m/2}_{\mathbb{Z},\sigma_{1}}\times(D_{\mathbb{Z}^{m/2},\sigma_{2},\delta_{1}}\times\ldots\times D_{\mathbb{Z}^{m/2},\sigma_{2},\delta_{\ell}})
, where
\delta_{i}\in Z^{\ell}
denotes the
i
-th canonical vector, and the
standard deviation parameters satisfy
\sigma_{1}=\Theta(\sqrt{n\log m}\max(\sqrt{m},K^{\prime}))
and
\sigma_{2}=\Theta(n^{7/2}m{1/2}\max(m,K^{\prime 2})\log^{5/2}m)
, with
K^{\prime}=(\sqrt{\ell}p)^{\ell}
.

Further,
R^{\prime}_{{\sf ISIS}}
-robustness (
Lemma

7.5
) will require the constraint
\alpha^{-1}\geq 4p\omega(\sqrt{\log n})(\ell p+m\beta_{1})
, where
\beta_{1}
is as chosen below.

Lemma 7.3

(
[
ALS16
]
)
.

Suppose
\ell\leq n^{O(1)}
,
m\geq 4n\log_{2}(q)
and
q,\alpha,\tau
are as described above.
Suppose
{{\sf mheLWE}}_{q,\alpha,m,\ell,\tau}

assumption (
Definition

3.20
)
holds.
Then, the IPFE scheme in
Figure

14
is selective,
IND-secure (
Definition

3.11
).

Next, we show the IPFE scheme augmented with the above
{\sf PubKGen}
algorithm satisfies
R_{{\sf ISIS}}
-compliance and
R^{\prime}_{{\sf ISIS}}
-robustness,
where
R_{{\sf ISIS}}={{\sf ISIS}}_{n,m,q,\beta_{0}}
and
R^{\prime}_{{\sf ISIS}}={{\sf ISIS}}_{n,m,q,\beta_{1}}
, where
\beta_{0}=\ell pB_{\tau}
and
\beta_{1}=2(\gamma-\kappa)-\beta_{0}
s.t.
\beta_{1}>\beta_{0}
and
\gamma-\kappa-\beta_{0}>0
.

Lemma 7.4
.

The IPFE scheme in
Figure

14
is
R_{{\sf ISIS}}
-compliant,
where
R_{{\sf ISIS}}={{\sf ISIS}}_{n,m,q,\beta_{0}}
and
\beta_{0}=\ell pB_{\tau}
.

Proof.

For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
for any
\mathsf{pp}\leftarrow{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda})
as implemented in
Figure

14
,
for any
({\sf mpk},{\sf msk})\leftarrow{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Setup}(\mathsf{pp},1^{\ell})
as implemented in
Figure

14
,
for any
{\bf y}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell,p}
,
let
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}:={\sf PubKGen}(\allowbreak{\sf mpk},{\bf y})
as implemented in
Figure

14
, and

\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}:={\sf KGen}({\sf msk},{\bf y})
as implemented in
Figure

14
.
Suppose here
{\sf mpk}=({\bf A},{\bf U})
and
{\sf msk}={\bf Z}
.
Then,
\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}={\bf Z}^{T}{\bf y}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
and
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}={\bf U}^{T}{\bf y}\bmod{q}={\bf A}^{T}\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}\bmod{q}
.
Further, note that
||\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}||_{\infty}\leq\ell\cdot||{\bf Z}||_{\infty}\cdot||{\bf y}||_{\infty}\leq\ell\cdot||{\bf Z}||_{2}\cdot||{\bf y}||_{\infty}\leq\ell\cdot B_{\tau}\cdot p=\beta_{0}
. Hence,
(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y},\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y})\in R_{{\sf ISIS}}
.
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

If
\alpha^{-1}\geq 4p\omega(\sqrt{\log n})(\ell p+m\beta_{1})
, then,
the IPFE scheme in
Figure

14
is
R^{\prime}_{{\sf ISIS}}
-robust,
where
R^{\prime}_{{\sf ISIS}}={{\sf ISIS}}_{n,m,q,\beta_{1}}
and
\beta_{1}=2(\gamma-\kappa)-\beta_{0}
such that
\beta_{1}>\beta_{0}
.

Proof.

For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
let
\mathsf{pp}\leftarrow{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda})

as implemented in
Figure

14
,
for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

{\bf x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell},{\bf y}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell,p}
,
let
({\sf mpk},{\sf msk})\leftarrow{\sf Setup}(\mathsf{pp},\ell)

as implemented in
Figure

14
,
let
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}={\sf PubKGen}({\sf msk},{\bf y})

as implemented in
Figure

14
,
let
{\sf ct}\leftarrow{\sf Enc}({\sf mpk},{\bf x})

as implemented in
Figure

14
.
Then, for any

\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{\bf y}
such that
(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y},\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{\bf y})\in R^{\prime}_{{\sf ISIS}}
,
we want that
{\sf Dec}(\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{\bf y},{\sf ct})
outputs
{\bf x}^{T}{\bf y}\bmod{q}
.
Observe that the value
d
computed by
{\sf Dec}

implemented in
Figure

14
simplifies to

d=p^{k-1}{\bf x}^{T}{\bf y}+({\bf e}_{1}^{T}{\bf y}-{\bf e}_{0}^{T}\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{\bf y})\bmod{q}
.
Observe that

\displaystyle|{\bf e}_{1}^{T}{\bf y}-{\bf e}_{0}^{T}\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}|

\displaystyle\leq\ell p\alpha q\omega(\sqrt{\log n})+m\alpha q\omega(\sqrt{\log n})||\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{\bf y}||_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\alpha q\omega(\sqrt{\log n})(\ell p+m\beta_{1}).

For decryption correctness to hold, we need that
|{\bf e}_{1}^{T}{\bf y}-{\bf e}_{0}^{T}\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}|\leq q/4p
. Hence it suffices to have
\alpha^{-1}\geq 4p\omega(\sqrt{\log n})(\ell p+m\beta_{1})
.
∎

7.3
\mathsf{FAS}
Construction

Instantiating
\mathsf{FAS}
construction in
Section

5
with
{{\sf IPFE}}
from
Figure

14
and
\mathsf{AS}
from
Figure

13
, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 7.6
.

Let
p
be a
\lambda
-bit prime number and let
\ell
be an integer.
Let
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}
be an additive group.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell,p}
be the function family for computing inner products of vectors in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}
.
Let
R
be any NP relation
with statement/witness pairs
(X,{\bf x})
such that
{\bf x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}
.
Let
R_{{\sf ISIS}}={{\sf ISIS}}_{n,m,q,\beta_{0}}
and
R^{\prime}_{{\sf ISIS}}={{\sf ISIS}}_{n,m,q,\beta_{1}}
, where
\beta_{0}=\ell pB_{\tau}
and
\beta_{1}=2(\gamma-\kappa)-\beta_{0}
s.t.
\beta_{1}>\beta_{0}
and
\gamma-\kappa-\beta_{0}>0
.
Suppose that

•

All function queries
{\bf y}
to
{\mathsf{AuxGen}}
are linearly independent modulo
p
,

•

R
is
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell,p}
-hard (
Definition

3.2
),

•

{\mathsf{NIZK}}
is a secure NIZK argument system (
Definition

3.9
),

•

\mathsf{AS}
construction in
Figure

13
is an adaptor signature scheme w.r.t. digital signature scheme
\mathsf{Lyu^{\prime}}
and hard relations
R_{{\sf ISIS}}
and
R^{\prime}_{{\sf ISIS}}
that satisfies weak pre-signature adaptability and witness extractability (
Lemma

7.1
),

•

{{\sf IPFE}}
construction in
Figure

11
is a selective, IND-secure IPFE scheme (
Lemma

7.3
) for function family
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell+1}
that is
R_{{\sf ISIS}}
-compliant (
Lemma

7.4
) and
R^{\prime}_{{\sf ISIS}}
-robust (
Lemma

7.5
).

Then, the functional adaptor signature scheme
w.r.t. Lyubashevsky signature scheme
\mathsf{Lyu^{\prime}}
, NP relation
R
, and family of inner product functions
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell,p}

constructed in
Figure

2
is strongly-secure (
Definition

4.3
).

The proof of the above corollary is immediate from the proof of the
Theorem

5.2
concerning the generic construction, and
Lemmas

7.3
,
7.4
and
7.5
that show that the
{{\sf IPFE}}
scheme in
Figure

14
has the required properties.

Remark 7.7
.

Note that the linear independence modulo
p
restriction for all
{\mathsf{AuxGen}}
queries in this lattice-based instantiation comes due to the same restriction on the underlying IPFE scheme as discussed in
Remark

7.2
. In practice,
{\mathsf{AuxGen}}
queries can be sent by any buyer to the seller. On one hand it’s reasonable to assume that all the queries by a single buyer are going to be linearly independent modulo
p
because by linearity of the functionality, the buyer can locally compute the function evaluation for some function
{\bf y}
that is linear combination of the functions
{\bf y}_{i}
’s it previously queried as follows: if
{\bf y}=\sum_{i}k_{i}{\bf y}_{i}\bmod{q}
, then,
f_{\bf y}({\bf x})
can be computed as
f_{\bf y}({\bf x}):=\sum_{i}k_{i}f_{{\bf y}_{i}}({\bf x})
. On the other hand, it’s unreasonable to assume that all buyers are working in coordination and make sure that their queries are linearly independent. In
Section

7.4
, we show how to remove this restriction on FAS.

7.4
Modifying
\mathsf{FAS}
Construction in
Figure

2
.

There is a simple way to ensure that even if
{\mathsf{AuxGen}}
queries across multiple buyers are linearly dependent modulo
p
, the requests to the underlying
{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf PubKGen}
are always linearly independent. Assume a polynomial bound
k
on the number of buyers. Then, we add
k
additional slots to the underlying IPFE resulting in a total of
\ell_{k}+1
slots and modify the
\mathsf{FAS}
construction as follows:

•

In
{\mathsf{AdGen}}
on input
{\bf x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}
, the vector
\widetilde{{\bf x}}:=({\bf x}^{T},{\bf 0}^{T})^{T}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell+k+1}
is encrypted using
{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Enc}
.

•

Further, the seller maintains an internal map of size
k
from buyer verification keys
vk
’s to a unique unit vector assigned to them. In other words,
i
-th buyer with verification key denoted by
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk}_{i}
is assigned
i
-th unit vector
{\bf e}_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{k}
.

•

At time of
{\mathsf{AuxGen}}
query for vector
{\bf y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}
by
i
-th buyer, the seller uses
\widetilde{{\bf y}}:=({\bf y}^{T},f_{\bf y}({\bf t}),{\bf e}_{i}^{T})^{T}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell+k+1}
to compute
{\sf aux}_{\bf y}
and sets
\pi_{\bf y}:=(f_{\bf y}({\bf t}),i)
.

•

In
{\mathsf{AuxVerify}}
, the buyer can recompute
\widetilde{{\bf y}}
on its own using
\pi_{\bf y}=(f_{\bf y}({\bf t}),i)
and return
1
iff
{\sf aux}_{\bf y}={{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf PubKGen}({\sf mpk},\widetilde{{\bf y}})
.

•

At the time of running
\mathsf{Adapt}
, the seller looks up
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk}
in its internal map to find the index
i
assigned to it. Then, it can recompute the same
\widetilde{{\bf y}}
that it used during
{\mathsf{AuxGen}}
query
{\bf y}
by buyer
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk}
. This will ensure that the adapted signature passes the signature verification.

Note that, the
k
extra slots as used above do not affect correctness as the function evaluation for a function
{\bf y}
is always
\widetilde{{\bf x}}^{T}\widetilde{{\bf y}}={\bf x}^{T}{\bf y}\bmod{q}
. Further note that these slots remain the same in all experiments for all security properties.

Remark 7.8

(Linear Independence)
.

Observe that with the above modification, even if two buyers
i
and
j
requested for the same function
{\bf y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}
,
{\mathsf{AuxGen}}
will use linearly independent queries
\widetilde{{\bf y}}_{i}
and
\widetilde{{\bf y}}_{j}
to the underlying
{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf PubKGen}
, where

\widetilde{{\bf y}}_{i}:=({\bf y}^{T},f_{\bf y}({\bf t}),{\bf e}_{i}^{T})^{T}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell+k+1}
and

\widetilde{{\bf y}}_{j}:=({\bf y}^{T},f_{\bf y}({\bf t}),{\bf e}_{j}^{T})^{T}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell+k+1}
. These two are linearly independent because for
i\neq j
, the unit vectors
{\bf e}_{i}
and
{\bf e}_{j}
are linearly independent of each other.

Remark 7.9

(Communication overhead)
.

Note that in the IPFE construction in
Figure

14
, the size of
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}
and
\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}
are independent of the vector length parameter
\ell
. Hence, change from
\ell+1
to
\ell+k+1
does not increase the size
{\sf aux}_{\bf y}
. The size of
\pi_{\bf y}
increases by
\log{k}
bits, but that can also optimized to be sent only once per buyer and not for every
{\mathsf{AuxGen}}
query. The main communication overhead is in the ciphertext
{\sf ct}
communicated as part of the advertisement
\mathsf{advt}
. Now
{\sf ct}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m+\ell_{k}+1}
instead of
{\sf ct}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m+\ell+1}
. But fortunately,
\mathsf{advt}
needs to be communicated only once.

8
Performance Evaluation

Our implementation aims to (i) show that FAS can replace smart contracts for efficient functional sales, and (ii) benchmark the computational costs for each functional sale.
We provide
an open-source implementation
[
VST24
]
of our prime-order group-based strongly-secure
\mathsf{FAS}
in Python.
We also perform a series of benchmarks on our implementation for a wide range of parameters.
Our results show that our scheme is practical for variety of real-world scenarios.

We measured the costs on a Apple MacBook Pro with M2 chip, 16GB memory, 8 cores (4 cores @3.49 GHz and 4 cores @2.42 GHz).
In typical applications, a seller wants to sell multiple functions of the same witness. The (application dependent) advertisement – containing NIZK proof – is just a one-time cost, while other processes are done once per sale. So, we benchmark computation costs of all algorithms except
{\mathsf{AdGen}}
and
{\mathsf{AdVerify}}
.

We use the
{\sf Secp256k1}
elliptic curve that is used by Bitcoin
[
sec
]
and other cryptocurrencies.
We use the curve’s python implementation from
{\sf hanabi1224}

[
han
]
.
For Schnorr signatures, we use a modified version
of BIP-340 reference implementation
[
bip
]
.
We implement the Schnorr adaptor signatures
[
AEE+21
]
on top of it.
For compliance purpose, we implement the IPFE scheme in
Figure

11

on the
{\sf Secp256k1}
curve.
Finally, using these adaptor signatures and IPFE schemes, we implement our functional adaptor signature construction.
We do not implement the NIZKs as they are needed only for
{\mathsf{AdGen}}
and
{\mathsf{AdVerify}}
which we do not implement here.

Optimizations.

We make following implementation optimizations.

•

For vectors of length
\ell
, we parallelize
{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Setup}
,
{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Enc}
.

•

Recall that
{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Dec}
computes
{\sf ct}_{1}^{T}{\bf y}-{\sf ct}_{0}^{T}\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}
(See
Figure

11
). Here,
{\sf ct}_{1}
and
{\bf y}
are vectors of length
\ell
and
{\sf ct}_{1}^{T}{\bf y}
is computed as
\prod_{i\in\ell}c_{i}^{y_{i}}
, where
{\sf ct}_{1}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell})
is a vector of group elements and
{\bf y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell})
is a vector of scalars. Thus, computing
{\sf ct}_{1}^{T}{\bf y}
is expensive as it involves
\ell
group exponentiation operations. But, this computation can be done in an offline stage by the buyer as it does not require knowledge of
\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}
from the seller. This helps us make
{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Dec}
and thus,
\mathsf{FAS}.{\mathsf{FExt}}
very efficient in practice.

•

Note that
{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf PubKGen}
and
{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Dec}
involve computing a product of powers such as
\prod_{i\in\ell}k_{i}^{y_{i}}
and
\prod_{i\in\ell}c_{i}^{y_{i}}
respectively. We compute these via
{\sf FastMult}
algorithm of
[
BGR98
, Section 3.2]
.

•

Note that
{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Dec}
involves a discrete log computation. For this task, we implement the baby-step giant-step algorithm
[
Sha71
]
.

•

Note that
{\mathsf{AuxGen}}
involves computing
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}:={{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf PubKGen}(\allowbreak{\sf mpk},\widetilde{{\bf y}})
which requires
\ell+1
group exponentiation operations. As
{\mathsf{AuxGen}}
is run by the seller who knows
\mathsf{st}=({\sf msk},{\bf t})
, hence, we optimize this computation as
\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}:={{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf KGen}({\sf msk},\widetilde{{\bf y}})
,
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}:=g^{\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}}
. This resulting way only involves
1
group exponentiation operation and hence is very efficient.

Communication efficiency.

In our implementation, each group element is 64 bytes elliptic curve point and each
Z_{p}
element is 32 bytes.
In
Table

1
, we give bounds on communication cost for a witness x of dimension
\ell=10^{6}
(total size 32 MB) and also specify if the communication is done on- or off-chain.

:

\mathsf{FAS}

\ell=10^{6}
.

Using standard compression techniques outlined in BIP-340
[
bip
]
, advertisement size can be reduced to 66 MB by encoding 64 bytes elliptic curve points using 33 bytes. In practice, the size of
\mathsf{advt}=({{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf mpk},{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf ct})
can be amortized to 33 MB by reusing the same
{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf mpk}
for all advertisements by a seller. The multi-message security of IPFE scheme should preserve FAS security and result in optimal amortized advertisement size of 1.03x the witness size.

Computational efficiency.

We share the performance numbers in
Table

2
.
Here,
\ell
denotes the length of the witness vector.
Each entry of the vector is 32 Bytes integer. Thus,
\ell=10^{6}
implies that the witness is of size 32MB.
We note that the pre-dominant cost in the implementation is the group exponentiation operation which hasn’t been optimized here.
We note that for parameter
\ell
, the number of group exponentiation operations in each algorithm are as follows:
in
{\mathsf{AuxGen}}
,

\ell+1
in
{\mathsf{AuxVerify}}
,

1
in
{\mathsf{FPreSign}}
,

\ell+2
in
{\mathsf{FPreVerify}}
,

0
in
\mathsf{Adapt}
,

\ell+2
in
{\mathsf{FExt}}
.
Asymptotically,
{\mathsf{AuxVerify}}
and
{\mathsf{FPreVerify}}
run in time
O(\ell)
and
{\mathsf{FExt}}
runs in time
O(\sqrt{\ell})
.
But, concretely
{\mathsf{FExt}}
is the slowest for practical scenarios as highlighted in
Table

2
.
The last two rows of
Table

2
do not highlight practical scenarios as the costs of these algorithms are higher than
100
seconds each, but we benchmark them to understand at what point do the concrete running times of
{\mathsf{AuxVerify}}
and
{\mathsf{FPreVerify}}
start becoming a bottleneck.

Appendix A
Proofs for Generic Construction of FAS

Here we present the formal proofs for the generic FAS construction from
Section

5
.

A.1
Correctness

First, we give the proof of correctness.

Proof of
Lemma

5.1
.

For every

\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, every message
m\in\{0,1\}^{*}
,
every statement/witness pair
(X,{\bf x})\in R
,
and every function
{\bf y}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
, suppose

\mathsf{pp}\leftarrow{\sf Setup}(1^{\lambda}),(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{st})\leftarrow{\mathsf{AdGen}}(\mathsf{pp},X,{\bf x}),\allowbreak(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk})\leftarrow{\sf KGen}(1^{\lambda}),({\sf aux}_{\bf y},\pi_{\bf y})\leftarrow{\mathsf{AuxGen}}(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{st},{\bf y}),\widetilde{\sigma}\leftarrow\allowbreak{\mathsf{FPreSign}}(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{sk},

m,X,{\bf y},{\sf aux}_{\bf y}),\sigma:=\mathsf{Adapt}(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{st},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,X,\allowbreak{\bf x},{\bf y},{\sf aux}_{\bf y}\widetilde{\sigma}),z:={\mathsf{FExt}}

(\mathsf{advt},\widetilde{\sigma},\sigma,X,{\bf y},{\sf aux}_{\bf y})
. Then,

•

From NIZK correctness, it follows that
{\mathsf{AdVerify}}(\mathsf{pp},X,\mathsf{advt})=1
.

•

For determinism of
{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf PubKGen}
, it follows that
{\mathsf{AuxVerify}}(\mathsf{advt},

{\bf y},{\sf aux}_{\bf y},\pi_{\bf y})=1
.

•

From correctness of
\mathsf{AS}
, it follows that
\ {\mathsf{FPreVerify}}(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,X,

{\bf y},{\sf aux}_{\bf y},\pi_{\bf y},\widetilde{\sigma})=1
.

•

Recall that
\widetilde{\sigma}
is an
\mathsf{AS}
pre-signature w.r.t. the statement
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}
.
Further, recall that
\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}
is the witness computed by the
\mathsf{Adapt}
algorithm and used by
\mathsf{AS}.\mathsf{Adapt}
to compute
\sigma
.
From
R_{{\sf IPFE}}
-compliance of IPFE, it follows that
(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y},\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y})\in R_{{\sf IPFE}}
. Hence, by the correctness of
\mathsf{AS}
, it follows that

{{\sf Vf}}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,\sigma)=1
.

•

From
\mathsf{AS}
correctness, it follows that
(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y},z)\in R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
, where
z
is the extracted witness in the
{\mathsf{FExt}}
algorithm. Then, from
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
-robustness of IPFE, it follows that
v=f_{\bf y}({\bf x})
, where
v
is output of
{\mathsf{FExt}}
.

∎

In the subsequent sub-sections, we prove the lemmas regarding advertisement soundness, unforgeability, pre-signature adaptability, witness extractability, zero-knowledge.

A.2
Advertisement soundness

Lemma A.1
.

Suppose NIZK satisfies adaptive soundness. Then, the functional adaptor signature construction in
Figure

2
is advertisement sound.

Proof.

We show that if a
p.p.t.
adversary
{\cal A}
breaks the advertisement soundness of our functional adaptor signature construction with non-negligible advantage,
then, there exists a
p.p.t.
reduction
{\cal B}
that can break the adaptive soundness of the underlying NIZK scheme with the same non-negligible advantage.
Suppose the challenger for adaptive soundness of NIZK is
{\cal C}
.
The reduction
{\cal B}
is as follows.

•

The reduction
{\cal B}
obtains
{\sf crs}
from the challenger
{\cal C}
, computes
\mathsf{pp}^{\prime}\leftarrow{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda})
, and sends
\mathsf{pp}=({\sf crs},\mathsf{pp}^{\prime})
to
{\cal A}
.

•

The adversary
{\cal A}
sends public advertisement
\mathsf{advt}
and a statement
X
to the reduction
{\cal B}
.

{\cal B}
parses
\mathsf{advt}=({\sf mpk},{\sf ct},\pi)
and sends the statement
(X,\mathsf{pp},{\sf mpk},{\sf ct})
and proof
\pi
to the challenger
{\cal C}
.

Observe that if
{\cal A}
breaks advertisement soundness, then,

X\notin L_{R}
and
{\mathsf{AdVerify}}(\mathsf{pp},X,\mathsf{advt})=1
.
From the definition of
L_{\mathsf{NIZK}}
it follows that if
X\notin L_{R}
, then,
(X,\mathsf{pp},{\sf mpk},{\sf ct})\notin L_{\mathsf{NIZK}}
.
Further, recall that the implementation of
{\mathsf{AdVerify}}
simply involves a
{\mathsf{NIZK}}
proof verification.
Therefore, if
{\mathsf{AdVerify}}(\mathsf{pp},X,\mathsf{advt})=1
, then,
{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{{\sf Vf}}({\sf crs},(X,\mathsf{pp},{\sf mpk},{\sf ct}),\pi)=1
.
Hence, whenever
{\cal A}
breaks advertisement soundness of
\mathsf{FAS}
,
{\cal B}
breaks adaptive soundness of
\mathsf{NIZK}
.

∎

A.3
Pre-Signature Validity

Lemma A.2
.

Suppose the adaptor signature scheme satisfies correctness (
Definition

3.6
).
Then, the functional adaptor signature construction in
Figure

2
satisfies pre-signature validity.

Proof.

For
any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
any
\mathsf{pp}\leftarrow{\sf Setup}(1^{\lambda})
as computed in
Figure

2
,
any
X,\mathsf{advt}
such that
{\mathsf{AdVerify}}(\mathsf{pp},\mathsf{advt},X)=1
as computed in
Figure

2
,
any message
m\in\{0,1\}^{*}
,
any function
{\bf y}\in\mathcal{F_{\sf IP},\ell}
,
any
({\sf aux}_{\bf y},\pi_{\bf y})
,
any key pair
(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk})\leftarrow{\sf KGen}(1^{\lambda})
,
any pre-signature
\widetilde{\sigma}\leftarrow{\mathsf{FPreSign}}(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{sk},m,X,{\bf y},{\sf aux}_{\bf y})
as computed in
Figure

2
,
suppose
{\mathsf{AuxVerify}}(\mathsf{advt},{\bf y},{\sf aux}_{\bf y},\pi_{\bf y})=1
.
Then, to prove that
\Pr[{\mathsf{FPreVerify}}(\allowbreak\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,X,f,{\sf aux}_{f},\pi_{f},\widetilde{\sigma})]=1
(as computed in
Figure

2
), it suffices to show that
\Pr[\mathsf{AS}.\mathsf{PreVerify}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},\allowbreak m,{\sf aux}_{\bf y},\widetilde{\sigma})]=1
. This follows from the correctness of the adaptor signature scheme
\mathsf{AS}
.
∎

A.4
Unforgeability

Lemma A.3
.

Suppose the NIZK argument system
{\mathsf{NIZK}}
satisfies adaptive soundness ((
Definition

3.9
)),
the Adaptor Signature scheme
\mathsf{AS}
satisfies witness extractability (
Definition

3.7
),
the relation
R
is a
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
-hard (
Definition

3.2
), and

{{\sf IPFE}}
scheme satisfies correctness and
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
-robustness (
Definition

3.14
).
Then, the functional adaptor signature instantiation above is
\mathsf{faEUF{\mathchar 45\relax}CMA}
-secure.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by defining a sequence of games.

Game
G_{0}
:

It is the original game
\mathsf{faEUF{\mathchar 45\relax}CMA}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}
, where the adversary
{\cal A}
has to come up with a valid forgery on a message
m^{*}
of his choice, while having access to functional pre-sign oracle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{fpS}}
and sign oracle
\mathcal{O}_{S}
.
Game
G_{0}
is formally defined in
Figure

15
.

Game
G_{1}
:

same as game
G_{0}
, except that
before computing the pre-signature,
the game checks if the NIZK statement
(X^{*},\mathsf{pp}^{\prime},{\sf mpk},{\sf ct})
is in the language
L_{NIZK}
.
If no, the game sets the flag
{\sf Bad}_{1}=\mathsf{true}
.
Game
G_{1}
is formally defined in
Figure

15
.

Game
G_{2}
:

same as game
G_{1}
, except that
when
{\cal A}
outputs the forgery
\sigma^{*}
,
the game extracts the witness
z
of the underlying adaptor signature scheme for the statement
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{{\bf y}^{*}}
and checks if the NIZK statement
({\sf aux}_{\bf y}^{*},z)
satisfies
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
.
If no, the game sets the flag
{\sf Bad}_{2}=\mathsf{true}
.
Game
G_{2}
is formally defined in
Figure

15
.

Unforgeability Proof: Games
G_{0},G_{1},G_{2}

To prove the lemma, we need to show that

\Pr[G_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda).

Note that by triangle inequality, it follows that

\displaystyle\Pr[G_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]

\displaystyle\leq|\Pr[G_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]-\Pr[G_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1]|

\displaystyle\quad\quad+|\Pr[G_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1]-\Pr[G_{2}(1^{\lambda})=1]|

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\Pr[G_{2}(1^{\lambda})=1].

To complete the proof,
we show in
Claims

A.4
,
A.5
and
A.6

that each of the three terms on the right-hand-side are at most
{\sf negl}(\lambda)
.
∎

Claim A.4
.

If the NIZK argument system
{\mathsf{NIZK}}
satisfies adaptive soundness (
Definition

3.9
), then,

|\Pr[G_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]-\Pr[G_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1]|\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

Observe that in game
G_{0}
,
{\sf Bad}_{1}=\mathsf{false}
always and game
G_{1}
differs from it when the flag
{\sf Bad}_{1}=\mathsf{true}
is set. Both games are identical until
G_{1}
checks the condition for setting
{\sf Bad}_{1}
.
Hence,

|\Pr[G_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]-\Pr[G_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1]|\leq\Pr[{\sf Bad}_{1}\text{ in $G_{1}$}].

Hence, it suffices to show that in game
G_{1}
,
\Pr[{\sf Bad}_{1}]\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda)
.

We prove the claim using a reduction to the adaptive soundness of the underlying
{\mathsf{NIZK}}
scheme. More specifically, we show that if the adversary
{\cal A}
causes game
G_{1}
to set
{\sf Bad}_{1}=\mathsf{true}
, then, we can use it to construct a reduction
{\cal B}
that breaks the adaptive soundness of
{\mathsf{NIZK}}
. Let
{\cal C}
be the challenger for the NIZK adaptive soundness. Then, the reduction is as in
Figure

16
.

{\cal B}
that breaks the witness extractability of

Figure 18
:
Reduction
{\cal B}
for proof of
Claim

A.6

We prove the claim using a reduction to the
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
-hardness of the relation
R
.
More specifically,
we show that if an adversary
{\cal A}
causes game
G_{2}
to return
1
, then,
we can use it to construct a reduction
{\cal B}
that breaks
the
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
-hardness of the relation
R
.
Let
{\cal C}
be the challenger for
the
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
-hardness of the relation
R
.
The reduction
{\cal B}
is as in
Figure

18
.

Observe that the reduction
{\cal B}
perfectly simulates game
G_{2}
to the adversary
{\cal A}
as it obtains
X^{*}
from the challenger
{\cal C}
who computes it as
(X^{*},\cdot)\leftarrow\mathsf{GenR}(1^{\lambda})
. Then, if
{\cal A}
causes
G_{2}
to return
1
, it must be the case that the if condition in step
18
of the reduction must be true. In such a case, the reduction returns
({\bf y}^{*},v)
to the challenger
{\cal C}
. To complete the proof, it remains to show that this results in
{\cal B}
winning the game against
{\cal C}
.

To win the game against
{\cal C}
, the reduction
{\cal B}
’s output must satisfy
({\bf y}^{*}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell})\wedge(v\in\{f_{{\bf y}^{*}}({\bf x}):\exists{\bf x}\ s.t.\ (X,{\bf x})\in R\})
.
Note that since the reduction did not abort, it implies
{\bf y}^{*}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
and
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{{\bf y}^{*}}={\sf aux}_{\bf y}^{*}
.
Next,
{\sf Bad}_{1}=\mathsf{false}
implies that
\mathsf{stmt}\in L_{NIZK}
, where
\mathsf{stmt}=(X^{*},\mathsf{pp}^{\prime},{\sf mpk},{\sf ct})
. Hence, it follows that
{\sf ct}
encrypts some vector
\widetilde{{\bf x}}=({\bf x}^{T},0)^{T}\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{\ell+1}
under
{\sf mpk}
such that
(X^{*},{\bf x})\in R
, where
({\sf mpk},{\sf msk})\leftarrow{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Setup}(\mathsf{pp}^{\prime},1^{\ell+1})
. Next,
{\sf Bad}_{2}=\mathsf{false}
implies that
({\sf aux}_{\bf y}^{*},z)\in R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
.
As
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{{\bf y}^{*}}={\sf aux}_{\bf y}^{*}
and IPFE satisfies
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
-robustness, it follows that
v=f_{\widetilde{{\bf y}^{*}}}(\widetilde{{\bf x}})
. As
\widetilde{{\bf y}^{*}}=({{\bf y}^{*}}^{T},\pi_{\bf y}^{*})^{T}
and
\widetilde{{\bf x}}=({\bf x}^{T},0)^{T}
, we get that
f_{\widetilde{{\bf y}^{*}}}(\widetilde{{\bf x}})=f_{{\bf y}^{*}}({\bf x})
.
Hence, we conclude that
v\in\{f_{{\bf y}^{*}}({\bf x}):\exists{\bf x}\ s.t.\ (X,{\bf x})\in R\}
. This completes the proof.

∎

\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{fpS}}
and sign oracle
\mathcal{O}_{S}
.

Figure

.

when

outputs a public advertisement

.

{\sf Bad}_{1}=\mathsf{true}
.
Game
G_{1}

Figure

.

To prove the lemma, we need to show that

\Pr[G_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda).

Note that by triangle inequality, it follows that

\Pr[G_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]\leq|\Pr[G_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]-\Pr[G_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1]|+\Pr[G_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1].

To complete the proof,
we show in
Claims

A.8
and
A.9

that each of the two terms on the right-hand-side are at most
{\sf negl}(\lambda)
.
∎

Claim A.8
.

If the NIZK argument system
{\mathsf{NIZK}}
satisfies adaptive soundness, then,

|\Pr[G_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]-\Pr[G_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1]|\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

Similar to proof of
Claim

A.4
.
∎

Reduction
B
for proof of
Claim

A.9

From the description of reduction
{\cal B}
,
we can observe that whenever the challenger
{\cal C}
obtains the signature
\sigma^{*}
on message
m^{*}
obtained, it is the case that
((m^{*}\notin\mathcal{Q})\wedge{{\sf Vf}}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m^{*},\sigma^{*})\wedge\neg{\sf Bad}_{0}\wedge\neg{\sf Bad}_{1}\wedge\neg{\sf Bad}_{2})
.
Suppose that the query set maintained by
{\cal C}
is denoted by
\mathsf{AS}.\mathcal{Q}
. For
{\cal B}
to succeed,
the signature
\sigma^{*}
must satisfy
((m^{*}\notin\mathsf{AS}.\mathcal{Q})\wedge{{\sf Vf}}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m^{*},\sigma^{*})\wedge((\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{{\bf y}^{*}},z)\notin R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}))
.
We note that
m^{*}\notin\mathcal{Q}
implies
m^{*}\notin\mathsf{AS}.\mathcal{Q}
as from the description of the reduction
{\cal B}
, it follows that
\mathcal{Q}=\mathsf{AS}.\mathcal{Q}
.
Note that
\sigma^{*}
already satisfies the second condition and it suffices to show that if
\neg{\sf Bad}_{0}\wedge\neg{\sf Bad}_{1}\wedge\neg{\sf Bad}_{2}
, then,
(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{{\bf y}^{*}},z)\notin R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
.

Note that
{\sf Bad}_{0}=\mathsf{false}
implies
{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{{\sf Vf}}({\sf crs},\mathsf{stmt},\pi)=1
and
{\bf y}^{*}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
and
{\sf aux}_{\bf y}^{*}=\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{{\bf y}^{*}}
.
Next,
{\sf Bad}_{1}=\mathsf{false}
implies that
\mathsf{stmt}\in L_{\mathsf{NIZK}}
, where
\mathsf{stmt}=(X^{*},\mathsf{pp}^{\prime},{\sf mpk},{\sf ct})
. Hence, it follows that
{\sf ct}
encrypts some vector
\widetilde{{\bf x}^{*}}=({{\bf x}^{*}}^{T},0)^{T}\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{\ell+1}
under
{\sf mpk}
such that
(X^{*},{\bf x}^{*})\in R
, where
({\sf mpk},{\sf msk})\leftarrow{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Setup}(\mathsf{pp}^{\prime},1^{\ell})
. Next,
{\sf Bad}_{2}=\mathsf{false}
implies that
v\notin\{f_{{\bf y}^{*}}({\bf x}):\exists\ {\bf x}\ s.t.\ (X^{*},{\bf x})\in R\}
, where
v
is computed as
v:={{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Dec}(z,{\sf ct})
. This implies
v\neq f_{{\bf y}^{*}}({\bf x}^{*})
.
Note that for
\widetilde{{\bf y}^{*}}=({{\bf y}^{*}}^{T},\pi_{\bf y}^{*})^{T}
, we have
f_{\widetilde{{\bf y}^{*}}}(\widetilde{{\bf x}^{*}})=f_{{\bf y}^{*}}({\bf x}^{*})
.
Hence, it follows that
v\neq f_{\widetilde{{\bf y}^{*}}}(\widetilde{{\bf x}^{*}})
.

To complete the proof, we argue that if
v\neq f_{\widetilde{{\bf y}^{*}}}(\widetilde{{\bf x}^{*}})
, then,
(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{{\bf y}^{*}},z)\notin R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
. This follows from
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
-robustness of IPFE. In particular, the contra-positive form of
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
-robustness says that if
v\neq f_{\widetilde{{\bf y}^{*}}}(\widetilde{{\bf x}^{*}})
, then either
\mathsf{pp}^{\prime}
is not computed honestly, or
{\sf mpk}
is not computed honestly or
{\sf ct}
is not computed honestly or
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{{\bf y}^{*}}
is not computed honestly or
v
is not computed honestly or
(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{{\bf y}^{*}},z)\notin R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
. Observe that the challenger
{\cal C}
samples
\mathsf{pp}^{\prime}
honestly, the NIZK proof
\pi
attests that
{\sf mpk}
and
{\sf ct}
are computed honestly, the reduction
{\cal B}
computes
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{{\bf y}^{*}}
and
v
honestly. Thus, it must be the case that
(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{{\bf y}^{*}},z)\notin R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
.

∎

A.6
Pre-Signature Adaptability

Lemma A.10
.

Suppose
{{\sf IPFE}}
satisfies
R_{{\sf IPFE}}
-compliance (
Definition

3.13
) and suppose that
\mathsf{AS}
satisfies weak pre-signature adaptability (
Definition

3.8
).
Then, the functional adaptor signature construction in
Figure

2
is pre-signature adaptable (
Definition

4.9
).

Proof.

For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
{\sf crs}\leftarrow{\sf Setup}(1^{\lambda})

be as computed in
Figure

2
.
For any statement/witness pair
(X,{\bf x})\in R
,
let
(\mathsf{advt},

\mathsf{st})\leftarrow{\mathsf{AdGen}}(1^{\ell},{\sf crs},X,{\bf x})

be as computed in
Figure

2
.
For any message
m\in\{0,1\}^{*}
,
any function
{\bf y}\in\mathcal{F}_{IP,\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\ell}}
,
any
({\sf aux}_{\bf y},\pi_{\bf y})\leftarrow{\mathsf{AuxGen}}(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{st},{\bf y})
as computed in
Figure

2
,
any key pair
(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk})\leftarrow{\sf KGen}(1^{\lambda})

as computed in
Figure

2
,
any pre-signature
\widetilde{\sigma}\in\{0,1\}^{*}

such that
{\mathsf{FPreVerify}}(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,X,{\bf y},{\sf aux}_{\bf y},\pi_{\bf y},\widetilde{\sigma})=1
as computed in
Figure

2
.
This implies
\mathsf{AS}.\mathsf{PreVerify}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,{\sf aux}_{\bf y},\widetilde{\sigma})=1
.
By
R_{{\sf IPFE}}
-compliance of the
{{\sf IPFE}}
scheme,
we know that
({\sf aux}_{\bf y},\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y})\in R_{{\sf IPFE}}
, where
\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}
is as computed in the
\mathsf{Adapt}
algorithm in
Figure

2
.
Then, it follows by the weak pre-signature adaptability of
\mathsf{AS}
that

\Pr[\mathsf{AS}.{{\sf Vf}}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,\sigma)=1]=1
, where

\sigma=\mathsf{AS}.\mathsf{Adapt}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,{\sf aux}_{\bf y},\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y},\widetilde{\sigma})
.
Then, from the implementation of the
\mathsf{Adapt}
algorithm of our functional adaptor signature scheme as in
Figure

2
, it follows that

\Pr[{{\sf Vf}}(\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,\mathsf{Adapt}(\mathsf{advt},\mathsf{st},\mathsf{\vphantom{p}vk},m,X,{\bf x},{\bf y},{\sf aux}_{\bf y},\widetilde{\sigma}))=1]=1.

∎

A.7
Zero-Knowledge

Lemma A.11
.

Suppose
\mathcal{M}
is an additive group,
{\mathsf{NIZK}}
satisfies zero-knowledge (
Definition

3.9
) and
{{\sf IPFE}}
satisfies selective, IND-security (
Definition

3.11
).
Then, the functional adaptor signature construction in
Figure

2
is zero-knowledge (
Definition

4.10
).

Proof.

To prove the lemma, we need to show that for every stateful
p.p.t.
adversary
{\cal A}
,
there exists a stateful
p.p.t.
simulator
{\mathsf{Sim}}=({\sf Setup}^{*},{\mathsf{AdGen}}^{*},{\mathsf{AuxGen}}^{*},\mathsf{Adapt}^{*})
and
there exists a negligible function
{\sf negl}
such that
for all
p.p.t.
distinguishers
{\cal D}
,
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
for all
(X,{\bf x})\in R
,

|\Pr[{\cal D}(\mathsf{faZKReal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1]-\Pr[{\cal D}(\mathsf{faZKIdeal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{\mathsf{Sim}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1]|\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda).

We first describe the stateful simulator
{\mathsf{Sim}}=({\sf Setup}^{*},{\mathsf{AdGen}}^{*},\allowbreak{\mathsf{AuxGen}}^{*},\mathsf{Adapt}^{*})
.
Let
{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}=({\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\sf Setup}^{*},{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{{\sf Prove}}^{*})
be the NIZK simulator.
Then, the simulator
{\mathsf{Sim}}
is as in
Figure

21
.

,

:
Zero-Knowledge Simulator
{\mathsf{Sim}}

Having described the Simulator
{\mathsf{Sim}}
, the experiments
\mathsf{faZKReal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}
and
\mathsf{faZKIdeal}^{\mathsf{Sim}}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}
are as in
Figure

. Observe that
\mathsf{faZKIdeal}^{\mathsf{Sim}}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}
is same as
\mathsf{faZKReal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}
except that NIZK is switched to simulation mode and IPFE ciphertext
{\sf ct}
encrypts
\widetilde{{\bf x}}:=(-{\bf t}^{T},1)^{T}\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{\ell+1}
and functional keys
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}
and
\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}
correspond to
\widetilde{{\bf y}}:=({\bf y}^{T},f_{\bf y}({\bf t})+f_{\bf y}({\bf x}))^{T}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell+1}
.
Observe that
f_{\bf y}({\bf x})=f_{\widetilde{{\bf y}}}(\widetilde{{\bf x}})
.
To prove zero-knowledge, we introduce an intermediate hybrid experiment
{\sf Hyb}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}}
which is same as
\mathsf{faZKReal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}
except that only NIZK is switched to simulation mode.
Note that by triangle inequality, it follows that

\displaystyle|\Pr[{\cal D}(\mathsf{faZKReal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1]-\Pr[{\cal D}(\mathsf{faZKIdeal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{\mathsf{Sim}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1]|

\displaystyle\quad\leq|\Pr[{\cal D}(\mathsf{faZKReal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1]-\Pr[{\cal D}({\sf Hyb}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1]|

\displaystyle\quad\quad+|\Pr[{\cal D}({\sf Hyb}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1]-\Pr[{\cal D}(\mathsf{faZKIdeal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{\mathsf{Sim}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1]|.

To complete the proof, we show in
Claims

A.12
and
A.13
that each of the two terms on the right-hand-side are at most
{\sf negl}(\lambda)
.
∎

Claim A.12
.

Suppose
{\mathsf{NIZK}}
satisfies zero-knowledge.
Then, for every stateful
p.p.t.
adversary
{\cal A}
,
for the
p.p.t.
simulator
{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}
,
there exists a negligible function
{\sf negl}
such that
for all
p.p.t.
distinguishers
{\cal D}
,
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
for all
(X,{\bf x})\in R
,

|\Pr[{\cal D}(\mathsf{faZKReal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1]-\Pr[{\cal D}({\sf Hyb}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1]|\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda).

Proof.

Suppose towards a contradiction that for the stateful
p.p.t.
adversary
{\cal A}
that makes no queries to the oracles
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{AuxGen}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Adapt}}
, there exists a
p.p.t.
distinguisher
{\cal D}
and a non-negligible value
\epsilon
such that

|\Pr[{\cal D}(\mathsf{faZKReal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1]-\Pr[{\cal D}({\sf Hyb}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1]|=\epsilon.

Then, we build a
p.p.t.
distinguisher
{\cal D}_{\mathsf{NIZK}}
using
{\cal D}
which breaks the zero-knowledge of NIZK for the NP language
L_{\mathsf{NIZK}}
. This should complete the proof.

Suppose the inputs to the distinguisher
D_{\mathsf{NIZK}}
are the NIZK common reference string
{\sf crs}
and a NIZK proof
\pi
for some statement
(X,\mathsf{pp}^{\prime},{\sf mpk},{\sf ct})\in L_{\mathsf{NIZK}}
. Then,
{\cal D}_{\mathsf{NIZK}}
is as follows. It sets
\mathsf{pp}=({\sf crs},\mathsf{pp}^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{advt}=({\sf mpk},{\sf ct},\pi)
. As
{\cal A}
makes no oracle queries, the view of the adversary
{\cal A}
against the the zero-knowledge security of functional adaptor signatures is simply
(\mathsf{pp},\mathsf{advt},X)
.
Then,
{\cal D}_{\mathsf{NIZK}}
runs the distinguisher
{\cal D}
on inputs
(\mathsf{pp},\mathsf{advt},X)
and returns whatever
{\cal D}
returns.
Then, observe that

\displaystyle\Pr

\displaystyle\left[\begin{matrix}{\cal D}_{\mathsf{NIZK}}({\sf crs},\pi)=1:\\
{\sf crs}\leftarrow{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\sf Setup}(1^{\lambda}),\\
\pi\leftarrow{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{{\sf Prove}}(\mathsf{pp},(X,\mathsf{pp}^{\prime},{\sf mpk},{\sf ct}),(r_{0},r_{1},{\bf x}))\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr\left[\begin{matrix}{\cal D}(\mathsf{pp}=({\sf crs},\mathsf{pp}^{\prime}),\mathsf{advt}=({\sf mpk},{\sf ct},\pi),X)=1:\\
{\sf crs}\leftarrow{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\sf Setup}(1^{\lambda}),\\
\pi\leftarrow{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{{\sf Prove}}(\mathsf{pp},(X,\mathsf{pp}^{\prime},{\sf mpk},{\sf ct}),(r_{0},r_{1},{\bf x}))\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr\left[{\cal D}(\mathsf{faZKReal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1\right].

Similarly, we get that

\displaystyle\Pr

\displaystyle\left[\begin{matrix}{\cal D}_{\mathsf{NIZK}}({\sf crs},\pi)=1:\\
({\sf crs},\mathsf{td})\leftarrow{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\sf Setup}^{*}(1^{\lambda})\\
\pi\leftarrow{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{{\sf Prove}}^{*}({\sf crs},\mathsf{td},\mathsf{stmt}=(X,\mathsf{pp}^{\prime},{\sf mpk},{\sf ct}))\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr\left[\begin{matrix}{\cal D}(\mathsf{pp}=({\sf crs},\mathsf{pp}^{\prime}),\mathsf{advt}=({\sf mpk},{\sf ct},\pi),X)=1:\\
({\sf crs},\mathsf{td})\leftarrow{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\sf Setup}^{*}(1^{\lambda})\\
\pi\leftarrow{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{{\sf Prove}}^{*}({\sf crs},\mathsf{td},\mathsf{stmt}=(X,\mathsf{pp}^{\prime},{\sf mpk},{\sf ct}))\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr\left[{\cal D}({\sf Hyb}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1\right].

This implies that

\displaystyle\left|\begin{matrix}\Pr\left[\begin{matrix}{\cal D}_{\mathsf{NIZK}}({\sf crs},\pi)=1:\\
{\sf crs}\leftarrow{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\sf Setup}(1^{\lambda}),\\
\pi\leftarrow{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{{\sf Prove}}(\mathsf{pp},(X,\mathsf{pp}^{\prime},{\sf mpk},{\sf ct}),({\bf x},r))\end{matrix}\right]\\
-\Pr\left[\begin{matrix}{\cal D}_{\mathsf{NIZK}}({\sf crs},\pi)=1:\\
({\sf crs},\pi)\leftarrow{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{stmt}=(X,\mathsf{pp}^{\prime},{\sf mpk},{\sf ct}))\end{matrix}\right]\end{matrix}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\begin{matrix}\Pr\left[{\cal D}(\mathsf{faZKReal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1\right]\\
-\Pr\left[{\cal D}({\sf Hyb}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1\right]\end{matrix}\right|

\displaystyle=\epsilon.

As
\epsilon
is non-negligible, hence,
{\cal D}_{\mathsf{NIZK}}
breaks the zero-knowledge of the underlying NIZK scheme.

∎

Claim A.13
.

Suppose
\mathcal{M}
is an additive group and
{{\sf IPFE}}
satisfies selective, IND-security.
Then, for every stateful
p.p.t.
adversary
{\cal A}
,
for the
p.p.t.
simulators
{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}
and
{\mathsf{Sim}}
(described in
Figure

21
),
there exists a negligible function
{\sf negl}
such that
for all
p.p.t.
distinguishers
{\cal D}
,
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
for all
(X,{\bf x})\in R
,

|\Pr[{\cal D}({\sf Hyb}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1]-\Pr[{\cal D}(\mathsf{faZKIdeal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{\mathsf{Sim}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1]|\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda).

Proof.

Observe that experiments
{\sf Hyb}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}}
and
\mathsf{faZKIdeal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{\mathsf{Sim}}
differ in the following two aspects:

•

The vector
\widetilde{{\bf x}}
that ciphertext
{\sf ct}
encrypts:
In
{\sf Hyb}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}}
, we have

\widetilde{{\bf x}}:=({\bf x}^{T},0)^{T}\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{\ell+1}
,
and in
\mathsf{faZKIdeal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{\mathsf{Sim}}
, we have

\widetilde{{\bf x}}:=(-{\bf t}^{T},1)^{T}\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{\ell+1}
.

•

The vector
\widetilde{{\bf y}}
used for computing
\mathsf{\vphantom{p}pk}_{\bf y}
and
\mathsf{sk}_{\bf y}
for every
{\bf y}
queried to
{\mathsf{AuxGen}}
and
\mathsf{Adapt}
oracles:
In
{\sf Hyb}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}}
, we have

\widetilde{{\bf y}}:=({\bf y}^{T},f_{\bf y}({\bf t}))^{T}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell+1}
,
and in
\mathsf{faZKIdeal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{\mathsf{Sim}}
, we have

\widetilde{{\bf y}}:=({\bf y}^{T},f_{\bf y}({\bf t})+f_{\bf y}({\bf x}))^{T}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell+1}
.

Consider an intermediate hybrid
\overline{{\sf Hyb}}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}}
that uses
\widetilde{{\bf x}}
as in
{\sf Hyb}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}}
and
\widetilde{{\bf y}}
as in
\mathsf{faZKIdeal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{\mathsf{Sim}}
.

We argue that
{\sf Hyb}
and
\overline{{\sf Hyb}}
are identically distributed. The two differ in how
\widetilde{{\bf y}}
is computed.
Notice that by linearity, it follows that
f_{\bf y}({\bf t})+f_{\bf y}({\bf x})=f_{\bf y}({\bf t}+{\bf x})
. Hence, one can view the change from
{\sf Hyb}
to
\overline{{\sf Hyb}}
as simply a change of variables
\widetilde{{\bf t}}={\bf t}\to\widetilde{{\bf t}}={\bf t}+{\bf x}
.
As
\mathcal{M}
is an additive group and
{\bf t},{\bf x}\in\mathcal{M}
, hence,
{\bf t}+{\bf x}\in\mathcal{M}
.
As
{\bf t}{\overset{\$}{\leftarrow}}\mathcal{M}
, hence, it follows that
{\bf t}
and
{\bf t}+{\bf x}
are identically distributed.
Therefore,
{\sf Hyb}
and
\overline{{\sf Hyb}}
are identically distributed. In other words,

|\Pr[{\cal D}({\sf Hyb}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1]-\Pr[{\cal D}(\overline{{\sf Hyb}}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1]|=0.

To complete the proof, we argue that if
{{\sf IPFE}}
is selective, IND-secure, then,

|\Pr[{\cal D}(\overline{{\sf Hyb}}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1]-\Pr[{\cal D}(\mathsf{faZKIdeal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{\mathsf{Sim}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1]|\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda).

Suppose towards a contradiction that for any stateful
p.p.t.
adversary
{\cal A}
, there exists a
there exists a
p.p.t.
distinguisher
{\cal D}
and a non-negligible value
\epsilon
such that

|\Pr[{\cal D}(\overline{{\sf Hyb}}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{{\mathsf{NIZK}}.{\mathsf{Sim}}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1]-\Pr[{\cal D}(\mathsf{faZKIdeal}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}^{\mathsf{Sim}}(1^{\lambda},X,{\bf x}))=1]|=\epsilon.

Then, we build a
p.p.t.
reduction
{\cal B}
using
{\cal D}
that breaks the selective, IND-security of IPFE. This should complete the proof.

\got@maxcolwd

Reduction
{\cal B}
for proof of
Claim

A.13

Let the IPFE challenger be
{\cal C}
and let its
{\sf KGen}
oracle be denoted be
{{\sf IPFE}}.\mathcal{O}_{\sf KGen}
. The reduction
{\cal B}
is as in
Figure

24
.
Observe that if the challenger
{\cal C}
chooses to encrypt
\widetilde{{\bf x}_{0}}
, then,
{\cal A}
’s view is same as in
\overline{{\sf Hyb}}
and if it chooses to encrypt
\widetilde{{\bf x}_{1}}
, then
{\cal A}
’s view is same as in
\mathsf{faZKIdeal}
. Thus, if
{\cal D}
can distinguish the two views of
{\cal A}
with a non-negligible distinguishing advantage
\epsilon
against
{\cal B}
, then,
{\cal B}
has the same non-negligible distinguishing advantage
\epsilon
against
{\cal C}
. Thus,
{\cal B}
breaks the selective, IND-security of IPFE.

∎

Appendix B
Weakly-Secure
\mathsf{FAS}
Construction

Recall that our main
\mathsf{FAS}
construction was obtained by
starting with a simulation-secure IPFE scheme.
The construction was presented using an IND-secure IPFE and applying the IND-secure IPFE to simulation-secure IPFE compiler of
[
ALMT20
]
in a non-black-box way within our
\mathsf{FAS}
construction. Here, we show that if (i) we do not apply the compiler and just use the IND-secure IPFE, (ii) use NIZK with adaptive zero-knowledge, then, the resulting
\mathsf{FAS}
construction is weakly-secure. This means that it satisfies all security properties as before except that for witness privacy, it satisfies witness indistinguishability. We first define adaptive zero-knowledge of NIZK and then describe the FAS construction next.

Definition B.1

(Adaptively Secure NIZK Argument System)
.

An adaptively secure NIZK argument system must satisfy completeness and adaptive soundness as before. Additionally, it must satisfy
adaptive zero-knowledge
which requires that
there exists a

p.p.t.
simulator
{\mathsf{Sim}}=({\sf Setup}^{*},{{\sf Prove}}^{*})

and there exists a negligible function
{\sf negl}
such that
for all
p.p.t.
distinguishers
{\cal D}
,
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
for all
(\mathsf{stmt},\mathsf{wit})\in R
,

|\Pr[{\cal D}({\sf crs},\pi)=1]-\Pr[{\cal D}({\sf crs}^{*},\pi^{*})=1]|\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda),

where

{\sf crs}\leftarrow{\sf Setup}(1^{\lambda})
,

\pi\leftarrow{{\sf Prove}}({\sf crs},\mathsf{stmt},

\mathsf{wit})
,
and

({\sf crs}^{*},{\sf td})\leftarrow{\sf Setup}^{*}(1^{\lambda})
,

\pi\leftarrow{{\sf Prove}}^{*}({\sf crs}^{*},{\sf td},\mathsf{stmt})
.
Here,
{\sf td}
is the trapdoor for the simulated
{\sf crs}^{*}
that is used by
{{\sf Prove}}^{*}
to generate an accepting proof for
\mathsf{stmt}
without knowing the witness.

Our generic construction of weakly-secure functional adaptor signatures will use the following building blocks:

•

An inner product functional encryption scheme
{{\sf IPFE}}
that satisfies selective, IND-security (
Definition

3.11
),

R_{{\sf IPFE}}
-compliance (
Definition

3.13
) and

R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
-robustness (
Definition

3.14
)
w.r.t. hard relations
R_{{\sf IPFE}}
,
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
such that
R_{{\sf IPFE}}\subseteq R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
. The message space of
{{\sf IPFE}}
is
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
and the function family is
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
.

•

An adaptor signature scheme
\mathsf{AS}
w.r.t. a digital signature scheme
\mathsf{DS}
, and hard relations
R_{{\sf IPFE}}
and
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
that satisfies witness extractability (
Definition

3.7
) and weak pre-signature adaptability (
Definition

3.8
) security properties.

•

An adaptively secure
{\mathsf{NIZK}}
(
Definition

B.1
) for the NP language
L_{\mathsf{NIZK}}
defined as

L_{\mathsf{NIZK}}:=\left\{(X,\mathsf{pp}^{\prime},{\sf mpk},{\sf ct}):\begin{matrix}\exists(r_{0},r_{1},{\bf x})\ \text{such that}\\
\mathsf{pp}^{\prime}\in[{{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda})],\\
({\sf mpk},{\sf msk})={{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Setup}(\mathsf{pp}^{\prime},1^{\ell+1};r_{0}),\\
(X,{\bf x})\in R,\\
{\sf ct}={{\sf IPFE}}.{\sf Enc}({\sf mpk},{\bf x};r_{1})\end{matrix}\right\}.

Our construction is as in
Figure

25
. Observe that it uses
\ell
slots instead of
\ell+1
slots in the underlying
{{\sf IPFE}}
, thus making the construction slightly more efficient than before. Further, compared to before,
{\mathsf{AuxGen}}
and
{\mathsf{AuxVerify}}
are redundant, thus, can be skipped in the protocol resulting in a non-interactive pre-signing stage. The construction can be instantiated from prime-order groups and lattices as before.

Theorem B.3
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
be the function family for computing inner products of vectors of length
\ell
.
Let
R
be any NP relation
with statement/witness pairs
(X,{\bf x})
such that

for some set
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
.

•

\mathcal{M}
is an additive group,

•

is
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
-hard (
Definition

3.2
),

•

{\mathsf{NIZK}}
is a secure NIZK argument system (
Definition

3.9
),

•

\mathsf{AS}
is an adaptor signature scheme w.r.t. digital signature scheme
\mathsf{DS}
and hard relations
R_{{\sf IPFE}},R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
that satisfies weak pre-signature adaptability (
Definition

3.8
) and witness extractability (
Definition

3.7
),

•

{{\sf IPFE}}
is a selective, IND-secure IPFE scheme (
Definition

3.11
) for function family
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}
that is
R_{{\sf IPFE}}
-compliant (
Definition

3.13
) and
R^{\prime}_{{\sf IPFE}}
-robust (
Definition

3.14
).

Then, the functional adaptor signature scheme
w.r.t. digital signature scheme
\mathsf{DS}
, NP relation
R
, and family of inner product functions
\mathcal{F}_{{\sf IP},\ell}

constructed in
Figure

25
is weakly-secure (
Definition

4.4
).

Proof.

Follows from
Lemmas

A.1
,
A.2
,
A.3
,
A.7
,
A.10
and
B.4
.
∎

B.1
Witness Indistinguishability

Lemma B.4
.

Suppose
{\mathsf{NIZK}}
satisfies adaptive zero-knowledge and
{{\sf IPFE}}
satisfies selective, IND-security.
Then, the functional adaptor signature construction in
Figure

2
is witness indistinguishable.

Proof.

We proof this through a sequence of games
G_{0}^{b}
and
G_{1}^{b}
for
b\in\{0,1\}
.

This game corresponds to the original game

\mathsf{faWI}^{b}_{{\cal A},\mathsf{FAS}}

\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{AuxGen}}
as it is redundant in context of the construction.

This game is same as
G_{0}^{b}
except that NIZK simulator is used instead of NIZK prover.
Formally, the game is defined in
Figure

.

To prove the lemma, we need to show that

|\Pr[G^{0}_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]-\Pr[G^{1}_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]|\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda),

Note that by triangle inequality, it follows that

\displaystyle|\Pr[G^{0}_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]-\Pr[G^{1}_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]|

\displaystyle\quad\leq|\Pr[G^{0}_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]-\Pr[G^{0}_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1]|

\displaystyle\quad\quad+|\Pr[G^{0}_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1]-\Pr[G^{1}_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1]|

\displaystyle\qquad+|\Pr[G^{1}_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1]-\Pr[G^{1}_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]|.

To complete the proof,
we show in
Claim

B.5

that the first and third terms on the right-hand-side are

\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda)

and in
Claim

B.6

that the second term on the right-hand-side is

\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda)
.

∎

Claim B.5
.

If
{\mathsf{NIZK}}
satisfies adaptive zero-knowledge, then,
there exists a negligible function
{\sf negl}
such that for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
for all
b\in\{0,1\}
,

|\Pr[G^{b}_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]-\Pr[G^{b}_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1]|\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda).

Proof.

We show that if there exists a
p.p.t.
adversary
{\cal A}
can distinguish its view in games
G^{b}_{0}
and
G^{b}_{1}
, then, we can create a distinguisher
{\cal D}^{b}
that breaks the adaptive zero-knowledge of the underlying
{\mathsf{NIZK}}
. Let
{\cal C}
be the adaptive zero-knowledge challenger for
{\mathsf{NIZK}}
.
{\cal C}
samples a random bit
\beta{\overset{\$}{\leftarrow}}\{0,1\}
and if
\beta=0
, it samples
{\sf crs}
and
\pi
as in the real-world and if
\beta=1
, it samples
{\sf crs}
and
\pi
using the
{\mathsf{NIZK}}
simulator algorithms
{\sf Setup}^{*}
and
{{\sf Prove}}^{*}
respectively.
Note that as the adaptive zero-knowledge of the NIZK proof system holds for all valid statement-witness pairs, we will allow the distinguisher
{\cal D}^{b}
to choose a valid statement-witness pair adaptively after seeing
{\sf crs}
.
The distinguisher
{\cal D}^{b}
must output a bit at the end and it will use the adversary
{\cal A}
for this task.
The distinguisher
{\cal D}^{b}
interacts with
{\cal A}
and either presents it the view as in
G^{b}_{0}
or as in
G^{b}_{1}
and at the end
{\cal A}
outputs its guess bit
\beta^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
. The distinguisher
{\cal D}^{b}
is as in
Figure

27
.

:

{\cal D}^{b}

Claim

B.5

Observe that if
{\cal C}
chose bit
\beta
, then, the view observed by
{\cal A}
is that of game
G^{b}_{\beta}
.
Hence, it follows that

\displaystyle|\Pr[{\cal D}^{b}({\sf crs},\pi)=1|\beta=0]-\Pr[{\cal D}^{b}({\sf crs},\pi)=1|\beta=1]|

\displaystyle\quad=|\Pr[G^{b}_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]-\Pr[G^{b}_{0}(1^{\lambda})=1]|.

Hence, if
{\cal A}
has a noticeable distinguishing advantage
\epsilon
, then,
{\cal D}^{b}
also has a noticeable distinguishing advantage
\epsilon
in its game with challenger
{\cal C}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Claim B.6
.

If
{{\sf IPFE}}
satisfies selective, IND-security, then,
there exists a negligible function
{\sf negl}
such that for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,

|\Pr[G^{0}_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1]-\Pr[G^{1}_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1]|\leq{\sf negl}(\lambda).

Proof.

We show that if there exists a
p.p.t.
adversary
{\cal A}
can distinguish its view in games
G^{0}_{1}
and
G^{1}_{1}
, then, we can create a reduction
{\cal B}
that breaks the selective, IND-security of the underlying
{{\sf IPFE}}
.
For the
{{\sf IPFE}}
selective, IND-security game,
let
{\cal C}
be the challenger and let
{{\sf IPFE}}.\mathcal{O}_{\sf KGen}
be the key generation oracle that the reduction
{\cal B}
has access to.
The reduction
{\cal B}
is as in
Figure

28
.

Figure 28
:
Reduction
{\cal B}
for proof of
Claim

B.6

Observe that when
{\cal C}
plays game
{\sf IPFE\text{-}Expt\text{-}SEL}_{\cal A}^{b}(1^{\lambda},n)
with the reduction
{\cal B}
for some
b\in\{0,1\}
, then,
{\sf ct}
encrypts
{\bf x}_{b}
and thus,
{\cal A}
’s view is same as
G^{b}_{1}
.
Further, observe that
{\cal B}
makes query to the key generation oracle only if
f_{\bf y}({\bf x}_{0})=f_{\bf y}({\bf x}_{1})
. Thus,
{\cal B}
is an admissible adversary in the game with
{\cal C}
. Hence, it follows that for all
b\in\{0,1\}
,

\displaystyle\Pr[{\sf IPFE\text{-}Expt\text{-}SEL}_{\cal B}^{b}(1^{\lambda},n)=1]

\displaystyle=\Pr[b^{\prime}=1]

\displaystyle=\Pr[G^{b}_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1].

Therefore, we get that

\displaystyle|\Pr[{\sf IPFE\text{-}Expt\text{-}SEL}_{\cal A}^{0}(1^{\lambda},n)=1]-\Pr[{\sf IPFE\text{-}Expt\text{-}SEL}_{\cal A}^{1}(1^{\lambda},n)=1]|

\displaystyle\qquad=|\Pr[G^{0}_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1]-\Pr[G^{1}_{1}(1^{\lambda})=1]|.

Hence, if
{\cal A}
has a noticeable distinguishing advantage
\epsilon
in its game with reduction
{\cal B}
, then,
{\cal B}
also has a noticeable distinguishing advantage
\epsilon
in its game with challenger
{\cal C}
. This completes the proof.
∎
